  "chapters": [
    {
      "id": "CH_1",
      "number": "0",
      "title": "Pre-Algebra",
      "order": 0,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_1",
          "chapter_id": "CH_1",
          "number": "0.1",
          "title": "Integers",
          "order": 0,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 7,
              "page_end": 7,
              "text": "0.1 \nPre-Algebra - Integers\nObjective: Add, Subtract, Multiply and Divide Positive and Negative\nNumbers. \nThe ability to work comfortably with negative numbers is ess ential to success in\nalgebra. For this reason we will do a quick review of adding, s ubtracting, multi-\nplying and dividing of integers. Integers are all the positive whole numbers, zero, \nand their opposites (negatives). As this is intended to be a r eview of integers, the\ndescriptions and examples will not be as detailed as a normal lesson. \nWorld View Note: The ﬁrst set of rules for working with negative numbers was\nwritten out by the Indian mathematician Brahmagupa. \nWhen adding integers we have two cases to consider. The ﬁrst i s if the signs\nmatch, both positive or both negative. If the signs match we w ill add the num-\nbers together and keep the sign. This is illustrated in the fo llowing examples\nExample 1. \n− 5 + ( − 3) Same sign, add 5 + 3, keep the negative\n− 8 Our Solution\nExample 2. \n− 7 + ( − 5) Same sign, add 7 + 5 , keep the negative\n− 12 Our Solution\nIf the signs don’t match, one positive and one negative numbe r, we will subtract\nthe numbers (as if they were all positive) and then use the sig n from the larger\nnumber. This means if the larger number is positive, the answ er is positive. If the\nlarger number is negative, the answer is negative. This is sh own in the following\nexamples. \nExample 3. \n− 7 + 2 Diﬀerent signs, subtract 7 − 2 , use sign from bigger number, negative\n− 5 Our Solution\nExample 4. \n− 4 + 6 Diﬀerent signs, subtract 6 − 4, use sign from bigger number, positive\n2 Our Solution\n7\nExample 5. \n4 + ( − 3) Diﬀerent signs, subtract 4 − 3, use sign from bigger number, positive\n1 Our Solution\nExample 6. \n7 + ( − 10) Diﬀerent signs, subtract 10 − 7, use sign from bigger number, negative\n− 3 Our Solution\nFor subtraction of negatives we will change the problem to an addition problem\nwhich we can then solve using the above methods. The way we cha nge a subtrac-\ntion to an addition is to add the opposite of the number after t he subtraction\nsign. Often this method is refered to as “add the opposite.” T his is illustrated in\nthe following examples. \nExample 7. \n8 − 3 Add the opposite of3\n8 + ( − 3) Diﬀerent signs, subtract 8 − 3, use sign from bigger number, positive\n5 Our Solution\nExample 8. \n− 4 − 6 Add the opposite of6\n− 4 + ( − 6) Same sign, add 4 + 6, keep the negative\n− 10 Our Solution\nExample 9. \n9 − ( − 4) Add the opposite of− 4\n9 + 4 Same sign, add 9 + 4, keep the positive\n13 Our Solution\nExample 10. \n− 6 − ( − 2) Add the opposite of− 2 \n− 6 + 2 Diﬀerent sign, subtract 6 − 2 , use sign from bigger number, negative\n− 4 Our Solution\n8"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_1"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Integers well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain what integers are and their importance in algebra.",
            "Add, subtract, multiply, and divide positive and negative numbers accurately.",
            "Apply the rules for working with integers to solve new problems without copying source material."
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_1_C1",
              "title": "What are Integers?",
              "explanation": "Integers include all positive whole numbers (1, 2, 3...), zero, and their opposites (negative numbers). They are essential to success in algebra because they allow us to work with quantities that have magnitude but no direction.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 7"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_2",
          "chapter_id": "CH_1",
          "number": "0.2",
          "title": "Fractions",
          "order": 1,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 12,
              "page_end": 12,
              "text": "0.2\nPre-Algebra - Fractions\nObjective: Reduce, add, subtract, multiply, and divide wit h fractions. \nWorking with fractions is a very important foundation to alg ebra. Here we will\nbrieﬂy review reducing, multiplying, dividing, adding, an d subtracting fractions. \nAs this is a review, concepts will not be explained in detail a s other lessons are. \nWorld View Note: The earliest known use of fraction comes from the Middle\nKingdom of Egypt around 2000 BC! \nWe always like our ﬁnal answers when working with fractions t o be reduced. \nReducing fractions is simply done by dividing both the numer ator and denomi-\nnator by the same number. This is shown in the following examp le\nExample 15. \n36\n84\nBoth numerator and denominator are divisible by4\n36 ÷ 4\n84 ÷ 4 = 9\n21 Both numerator and denominator are still divisible by3\n12\n9 ÷ 3\n21 ÷ 3 = 3\n7\nOur Soultion\nThe previous example could have been done in one step by divid ing both numer-\nator and denominator by 12. We also could have divided by 2 twi ce and then\ndivided by 3 once (in any order). It is not important which met hod we use as\nlong as we continue reducing our fraction until it cannot be r educed any further. \nThe easiest operation with fractions is multiplication. We can multiply fractions\nby multiplying straight across, multiplying numerators to gether and denominators\ntogether. \nExample 16. \n6\n7\n· 3\n5 \nMultiply numerators across and denominators across\n18\n35\nOur Solution\nWhen multiplying we can reduce our fractions before we multi ply. We can either\nreduce vertically with a single fraction, or diagonally wit h several fractions, as\nlong as we use one number from the numerator and one number fro m the denomi-\nnator. \nExample 17. \n25\n24\n· 32\n55\nReduce 25 and 55 by dividing by5 . Reduce 32 and 24 by dividing by8\n5 \n3\n· 4\n11 Multiply numerators across and denominators across\n20\n33\nOur Solution\nDividing fractions is very similar to multiplying with one e xtra step. Dividing\nfractions requires us to ﬁrst take the reciprocal of the seco nd fraction and mul-\ntiply. Once we do this, the multiplication problem solves ju st as the previous\nproblem. \n13"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_2"
          ],

            "Recognize when to use fractions later in the course"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_2_C1",
              "title": "What are Fractions?",
              "explanation": "Fractions represent parts of a whole, with a numerator (top number) and denominator (bottom number). They can be used to describe proportions or divisions of quantities.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 12"
              ],

                "SEC_1_C1"

        {
          "id": "SEC_3",
          "chapter_id": "CH_1",
          "number": "0.3",
          "title": "Order of Operations",
          "order": 2,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 18,
              "page_end": 18,
              "text": "0.3\nPre-Algebra - Order of Operations\nObjective: Evaluate expressions using the order of operati ons, including\nthe use of absolute value. \nWhen simplifying expressions it is important that we simpli fy them in the correct\norder. Consider the following problem done two diﬀerent way s: \nExample 24. \n2 + 5 \n· 3 Add First 2 + 5 · 3 Multiply\n7 · 3 Multiply 2 + 15 Add\n21 Solution 17 Solution\nThe previous example illustrates that if the same problem is done two diﬀerent\nways we will arrive at two diﬀerent solutions. However, only one method can be\ncorrect. It turns out the second method, 17, is the correct me thod. The order of\noperations ends with the most basic of operations, addition (or subtraction). \nBefore addition is completed we must do repeated addition or multiplication (or\ndivision). Before multiplication is completed we must do re peated multiplication\nor exponents. When we want to do something out of order and mak e it come ﬁrst\nwe will put it in parenthesis (or grouping symbols). This lis t then is our order of\noperations we will use to simplify expressions. \nOrder of Operations: \nParenthesis (Grouping) \nExponents\nMultiply and Divide (Left to Right) \nAdd and Subtract (Left to Right) \nMultiply and Divide are on the same level because they are the same operation\n(division is just multiplying by the reciprocal). This mean s they must be done left\nto right, so some problems we will divide ﬁrst, others we will multiply ﬁrst. The\nsame is true for adding and subtracting (subtracting is just adding the opposite). \nOften students use the word PEMDAS to remember the order of op erations, as\nthe ﬁrst letter of each operation creates the word PEMDAS. Ho wever, it is the\nauthor’s suggestion to think about PEMDAS as a vertical word written as: \nP\nE\nMD\nAS\nso we don’t forget that multiplication and division are done left to right (same\nwith addition and subtraction). Another way students remem ber the order of\noperations is to think of a phrase such as “Please Excuse My De ar Aunt Sally”\nwhere each word starts with the same letters as the order of op erations start with. \nWorld View Note: The ﬁrst use of grouping symbols are found in 1646 in the\nDutch mathematician, Franciscus van Schooten’s text, Viet a. He used a bar over\n18\nthe expression that is to be evaluated ﬁrst. So problems like 2(3 + 5) were written\nas 2 · 3 + 5 . \nExample 25. \n2 + 3(9− 4) 2 Parenthesis ﬁrst\n2 + 3(5) 2 Exponents\n2 + 3( 25) Multiply\n2 + 75 Add\n77 Our Solution\nIt is very important to remember to multiply and divide from f rom left to right! \nExample 26. \n30 ÷ 3 · 2 Divide ﬁrst ( left to right!) \n10 · 2 Multiply\n20 Our Solution\nIn the previous example, if we had multiplied ﬁrst, ﬁve would have been the\nanswer which is incorrect. \nIf there are several parenthesis in a problem we will start wi th the inner most\nparenthesis and work our way out. Inside each parenthesis we simplify using the\norder of operations as well. To make it easier to know which pa renthesis goes with\nwhich parenthesis, diﬀerent types of parenthesis will be us ed such as { } and [ ] \nand ( ), these parenthesis all mean the same thing, they are pa renthesis and must\nbe evaluated ﬁrst. \nExample 27. \n2 { 82 − 7[ 32 − 4(32 \n˜ + 1)]( − 1) } Inner most parenthesis, exponents ﬁrst\n2 { 82 − 7[ 32 − 4(9 + 1 \n)]( − 1) } Add inside those parenthesis\n2 { 82 − 7[ 32− 4( 10) ]( − 1) } Multiply inside inner most parenthesis\n2 { 82 − 7[ 32 − 40]( − 1) } Subtract inside those parenthesis\n2 { 82 \n˜ − 7[ − 8]( − 1) } Exponents next\n2 { 64− 7[ − 8] ( − 1) } Multiply left to right, sign with the number\n2 { 64 + 56( − 1) } Finish multiplying\n2 { 64 − 56} Subtract inside parenthesis\n2 { 8} Multiply\n16 Our Solution\nAs the above example illustrates, it can take several steps t o complete a problem. \nThe key to successfully solve order of operations problems i s to take the time to\nshow your work and do one step at a time. This will reduce the ch ance of making\na mistake along the way. \n19"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_3"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Order of Operations well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Understand the concept of Order of Operations",
            "Apply Order of Operations accurately in straightforward problems",
            "Explain the idea of Order of Operations in own words"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_3_C1",
              "title": "What is Order of Operations?",
              "explanation": "Order of Operations is a set of rules that tells us which mathematical operations to perform first when simplifying expressions. It ensures that we get the same solution regardless of the order in which we simplify an expression.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 18"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_4",
          "chapter_id": "CH_1",
          "number": "0.4",
          "title": "Properties of Algebra",
          "order": 3,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 22,
              "page_end": 22,
              "text": "0.4\nPre-Algebra - Properties of Algebra\nObjective: Simplify algebraic expressions by substitutin g given values, \ndistributing, and combining like terms\nIn algebra we will often need to simplify an expression to mak e it easier to use. \nThere are three basic forms of simplifying which we will revi ew here. \nWorld View Note: The term “Algebra” comes from the Arabic word al-jabr\nwhich means “reunion”. It was ﬁrst used in Iraq in 830 AD by Moh ammad ibn-\nMusa al-Khwarizmi. \nThe ﬁrst form of simplifying expressions is used when we know what number each\nvariable in the expression represents. If we know what they r epresent we can\nreplace each variable with the equivalent number and simpli fy what remains using\norder of operations. \nExample 30. \np( q + 6) when p = 3 and q = 5 Replace p with 3 and q with 5 \n(3) ((5) + 6) Evaluate parenthesis\n(3)( 11 ) Multiply\n33 Our Solution\nWhenever a variable is replaced with something, we will put t he new number\ninside a set of parenthesis. Notice the 3 and 5 in the previous example are in\nparenthesis. This is to preserve operations that are someti mes lost in a simple\nreplacement. Sometimes the parenthesis won’t make a diﬀere nce, but it is a good\nhabbit to always use them to prevent problems later. \nExample 31. \nx + zx(3 − z) \n( x\n3\n)\nwhen x = − 6 and z = − 2 Replace all x′s with 6 and z ′s with 2 \n( − 6) + ( − 2)( − 6) (3 − ( − 2)) \n( ( − 6) \n3\n)\nEvaluate parenthesis\n− 6 + ( − 2)( − 6) (5)( − 2) Multiply left to right\n− 6 + 12 (5) ( − 2) Multiply left to right\n− 6 + 60( − 2) Multiply\n− 6 − 120 Subtract\n− 126 Our Solution\n22\nIt will be more common in our study of algebra that we do not kno w the value of\nthe variables. In this case, we will have to simplify what we c an and leave the\nvariables in our ﬁnal solution. One way we can simplify expre ssions is to combine\nlike terms. Like terms are terms where the variables match exactly (exponents\nincluded). Examples of like terms would be 3x y and − 7x y or 3a2 b and 8a2 b or − \n3 and 5 . If we have like terms we are allowed to add (or subtract) the n umbers in\nfront of the variables, then keep the variables the same. Thi s is shown in the fol-\nlowing examples\nExample 32. \n5 x − 2 y − 8x + 7y Combine like terms5 x − 8x and − 2 y + 7y\n− 3x + 5 y Our Solution\nExample 33. \n8x2 − 3x + 7 − 2 x2 + 4x − 3 Combine like terms8x2 − 2 x2 and − 3x + 4x and 7 − 3\n6x2 + x + 4 Our Solution\nAs we combine like terms we need to interpret subtraction sig ns as part of the fol-\nlowing term. This means if we see a subtraction sign, we treat the following term\nlike a negative term, the sign always stays with the term. \nA ﬁnal method to simplify is known as distributing. Often as w e work with prob-\nlems there will be a set of parenthesis that make solving a pro blem diﬃcult, if not\nimpossible. To get rid of these unwanted parenthesis we have the distributive\nproperty. Using this property we multiply the number in fron t of the parenthesis\nby each term inside of the parenthesis. \nDistributive Property: a( b + c) = ab + ac\nSeveral examples of using the distributive property are giv en below. \nExample 34. \n4(2 x − 7) Multiply each term by4\n8x − 28 Our Solution\nExample 35. \n− 7(5 x − 6) Multiply each term by− 7\n− 35 + 42 Our Solution\nIn the previous example we again use the fact that the sign goe s with the number, \nthis means we treat the − 6 as a negative number, this gives ( − 7)( − 6) = 42, a\npositive number. The most common error in distributing is a s ign error, be very\ncareful with your signs! \n23"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_4"
          ],

            "Simplify algebraic expressions by substituting given values and combining like terms",
            "Apply the Distributive Property to remove unwanted parentheses"
          ],

    {
      "id": "CH_2",
      "number": "1",
      "title": "Solving Linear Equations",
      "order": 1,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_5",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.1",
          "title": "One-Step Equations",
          "order": 4,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 28,
              "page_end": 28,
              "text": "1.1 \nSolving Linear Equations - One Step Equations\nObjective: Solve one step linear equations by balancing usi ng inverse\noperations\nSolving linear equations is an important and fundamental sk ill in algebra. In\nalgebra, we are often presented with a problem where the answ er is known, but\npart of the problem is missing. The missing part of the proble m is what we seek\nto ﬁnd. An example of such a problem is shown below. \nExample 41. \n4x + 16 = − 4\nNotice the above problem has a missing part, or unknown, that is marked by x. If\nwe are given that the solution to this equation is − 5 , it could be plugged into the\nequation, replacing the x with − 5 . This is shown in Example 2. \nExample 42. \n4( − 5) + 16 = − 4 Multiply 4( − 5) \n− 20 + 16 = − 4 Add − 20 + 16\n− 4 = − 4 True! \nNow the equation comes out to a true statement! Notice also th at if another\nnumber, for example, 3, was plugged in, we would not get a true statement as\nseen in Example 3. \nExample 43. \n4(3) + 16 = − 4 Multiply 4(3) \n12 + 16 = − 4 Add 12 + 16\n28\n/\u0002 − 4 False! \nDue to the fact that this is not a true statement, this demonst ates that 3 is not\nthe solution. However, depending on the complexity of the pr oblem, this “guess\nand check” method is not very eﬃcient. Thus, we take a more alg ebraic approach\nto solving equations. Here we will focus on what are called “o ne-step equations” or\nequations that only require one step to solve. While these eq uations often seem\nvery fundamental, it is important to master the pattern for s olving these problems\nso we can solve more complex problems. \n28\nAddition Problems\nTo solve equations, the general rule is to do the opposite. Fo r example, consider\nthe following example. \nExample 44. \nx + 7 = − 5 The 7 is added to thex\n− 7 − 7\nSubtract 7 from both sides to get rid of it\nx = − 12 Our solution ! \nThen we get our solution, x = − 12. The same process is used in each of the fol-\nlowing examples. \nExample 45. \n4 + x = 8\n− 4 − 4\nx = 4\n7 = x + 9\n− 9 − 9 \n− 2 = x\n5 = 8 + x\n− 8 − 8 \n− 3 = x\nT able 1. Addition Examples\nSubtraction Problems\nIn a subtraction problem, we get rid of negative numbers by ad ding them to both\nsides of the equation. For example, consider the following e xample. \nExample 46. \nx − 5 = 4 The 5 is negative, or subtracted from x\n+ 5 + 5 \nAdd 5 to both sides\nx = 9 Our Solution! \nThen we get our solution x = 9. The same process is used in each of the following\nexamples. Notice that each time we are getting rid of a negati ve number by\nadding. \n29"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_5"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn One-Step Equations well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

            "Solve one-step linear equations using inverse operations",
            "Explain the concept of one-step equations and their importance in algebra",
            "Apply one-step equation solving to new problems without copying the source"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_5_C1",

        {
          "id": "SEC_6",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.2",
          "title": "Two-Step Equations",
          "order": 5,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 33,
              "page_end": 33,
              "text": "1.2\nLinear Equations - Two-Step Equations\nObjective: Solve two-step equations by balancing and using inverse\nopperations. \nAfter mastering the technique for solving equations that ar e simple one-step equa-\ntions, we are ready to consider two-step equations. As we sol ve two-step equa-\ntions, the important thing to remember is that everything wo rks backwards! \nWhen working with one-step equations, we learned that in ord er to clear a “plus\nﬁve” in the equation, we would subtract ﬁve from both sides. W e learned that to\nclear “divided by seven” we multiply by seven on both sides. T he same pattern\napplies to the order of operations. When solving for our vari able x, we use order\nof operations backwards as well. This means we will add or sub tract ﬁrst, then\nmultiply or divide second (then exponents, and ﬁnally any pa rentheses or\ngrouping symbols, but that’s another lesson). So to solve th e equation in the ﬁrst\nexample, \nExample 53. \n4x − 20 = − 8\nWe have two numbers on the same side as the x. We need to move the 4 and the\n20 to the other side. We know to move the four we need to divide, and to move\nthe twenty we will add twenty to both sides. If order of operat ions is done back-\nwards, we will add or subtract ﬁrst. Therefore we will add 20 t o both sides ﬁrst. \nOnce we are done with that, we will divide both sides by 4. The steps are shown\nbelow. \n4x − 20 = − 8 Start by focusing on the subtract 20\n+ 20 + 20\nAdd 20 to both sides\n4x = 12 Now we focus on the 4 multiplied by x\n4 4 Divide both sides by4\nx = 3 Our Solution! \nNotice in our next example when we replace the x with 3 we get a true state-\nment. \n4(3) − 20 = − 8 Multiply 4(3) \n12 − 20 = − 8 Subtract 12 − 20\n− 8 = − 8 True! \n33\nThe same process is used to solve any two-step equations. Add or subtract ﬁrst, \nthen multiply or divide. Consider our next example and notic e how the same pro-\ncess is applied. \nExample 54. \n5 x + 7 = 7 Start by focusing on the plus7\n− 7 − 7\nSubtract 7 from both sides\n5 x = 0 Now focus on the multiplication by5 \n5 5 Divide both sides by5 \nx = 0 Our Solution! \nNotice the seven subtracted out completely! Many students g et stuck on this\npoint, do not forget that we have a number for “nothing left” a nd that number is\nzero. With this in mind the process is almost identical to our ﬁrst example. \nA common error students make with two-step equations is with negative signs. \nRemember the sign always stays with the number. Consider the following\nexample. \nExample 55. \n4 − 2 x = 10 Start by focusing on the positive 4\n− 4 − 4\nSubtract 4 from both sides\n− 2 x = 6 Negative ( subtraction) stays on the2 x\n− 2 − 2 Divide by − 2 \nx = − 3 Our Solution! \nThe same is true even if there is no coeﬃcient in front of the va riable. Consider\nthe next example. \nExample 56. \n8 − x = 2 Start by focusing on the positive8\n− 8 − 8 \nSubtract 8 from both sides\n− x = − 6 Negative ( subtraction) stays on thex\n− 1 x = − 6 Remember, no number in front of variable means1 \n34"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_6"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Two-Step Equations well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Solve two-step equations by balancing and using inverse operations",
            "Explain the idea of two-step equations in own words",
            "Apply two-step equations to new problems without copying source"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_6_C1",
              "title": "Two-Step Equations",
              "explanation": "Two-step equations are a type of linear equation that requires two steps to solve. The first step involves moving a constant term to the other side of the equation, and the second step involves solving for the variable.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 33"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_7",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.3",
          "title": "General Linear Equations",
          "order": 6,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 37,
              "page_end": 37,
              "text": "1.3\nSolving Linear Equations - General Equations\nObjective: Solve general linear equations with variables o n both sides. \nOften as we are solving linear equations we will need to do som e work to set them\nup into a form we are familiar with solving. This section will focus on manipu-\nlating an equation we are asked to solve in such a way that we ca n use our pat-\ntern for solving two-step equations to ultimately arrive at the solution. \nOne such issue that needs to be addressed is parenthesis. Oft en the parenthesis\ncan get in the way of solving an otherwise easy problem. As you might expect we\ncan get rid of the unwanted parenthesis by using the distribu tive property. This is\nshown in the following example. Notice the ﬁrst step is distr ibuting, then it is\nsolved like any other two-step equation. \nExample 59. \n4(2 x − 6) = 16 Distribute 4 through parenthesis\n8x − 24 = 16 Focus on the subtraction ﬁrst\n+ 24 + 24\nAdd 24 to both sides\n8x = 40 Now focus on the multiply by 8\n8 8 Divide both sides by8\nx = 5 Our Solution! \nOften after we distribute there will be some like terms on one side of the equa-\ntion. Example 2 shows distributing to clear the parenthesis and then combining\nlike terms next. Notice we only combine like terms on the same side of the equa-\ntion. Once we have done this, our next example solves just lik e any other two-step\nequation. \nExample 60. \n3(2 x − 4) + 9 = 15 Distribute the 3 through the parenthesis\n6x − 12 + 9 = 15 Combine like terms , − 12 + 9\n6x − 3 = 15 Focus on the subtraction ﬁrst\n+ 3 + 3\nAdd 3 to both sides\n6x = 18 Now focus on multiply by 6\n37\n6 6 Divide both sides by6\nx = 3 Our Solution\nA second type of problem that becomes a two-step equation aft er a bit of work is\none where we see the variable on both sides. This is shown in th e following\nexample. \nExample 61. \n4x − 6 = 2 x + 10\nNotice here the x is on both the left and right sides of the equation. This can\nmake it diﬃcult to decide which side to work with. We ﬁx this by moving one of\nthe terms with x to the other side, much like we moved a constant term. It\ndoesn’t matter which term gets moved, 4x or 2 x, however, it would be the\nauthor’s suggestion to move the smaller term (to avoid negat ive coeﬃcients). For\nthis reason we begin this problem by clearing the positive 2 x by subtracting 2 x\nfrom both sides. \n4x − 6 = 2 x + 10 Notice the variable on both sides\n− 2 x − 2 x \nSubtract 2 x from both sides\n2 x − 6 = 10 Focus on the subtraction ﬁrst\n+ 6 + 6 Add 6 to both sides\n2 x = 16 Focus on the multiplication by 2 \n2 2 Divide both sides by2 \nx = 8 Our Solution! \nThe previous example shows the check on this solution. Here t he solution is\nplugged into the x on both the left and right sides before simplifying. \nExample 62. \n4(8) − 6 = 2(8) + 10 Multiply 4(8) and 2(8) ﬁrst\n32 − 6 = 16 + 10 Add and Subtract\n26 = 26 True ! \nThe next example illustrates the same process with negative coeﬃcients. Notice\nﬁrst the smaller term with the variable is moved to the other s ide, this time by\nadding because the coeﬃcient is negative. \n38"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_7"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn General Linear Equations well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

            "Explain the concept of general linear equations and their importance in solving problems",
            "Solve straightforward general linear equation problems using the distributive property and combining like terms",
            "Recognize when to use general linear equations in a new problem"
          ],

            {
              "id": "SEC_7_C1",
              "title": "General Linear Equations",
              "explanation": "A general linear equation is an equation that contains variables on both sides of the equals sign. It requires us to manipulate the equation using algebraic properties, such as the distributive property and combining like terms.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 37"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_8",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.4",
          "title": "Solving with Fractions",
          "order": 7,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 43,
              "page_end": 43,
              "text": "1.4\nSolving Linear Equations - Fractions\nObjective: Solve linear equations with rational coeﬃcient s by multi-\nplying by the least common denominator to clear the fraction s. \nOften when solving linear equations we will need to work with an equation with\nfraction coeﬃcients. We can solve these problems as we have i n the past. This is\ndemonstrated in our next example. \nExample 69. \n3\n4\nx − 7\n2 \n= 5 \n6\nFocus on subtraction\n+ 7\n2 \n+ 7\n2 \nAdd 7\n2 \nto both sides\nNotice we will need to get a common denominator to add 5\n6\n+ 7\n2 \n. Notice we have a\ncommon denominator of 6. So we build up the denominator, 7\n2 \n(\n3\n3\n)\n= 21 \n6 , and we\ncan now add the fractions: \n3\n4\nx − 21 \n6 = 5 \n6\nSame problem, with common denominator 6\n+ 21 \n6 + 21 \n6 Add 21 \n6 to both sides\n3\n4\nx = 26\n6 Reduce 26\n6 to 13\n3\n3\n4\nx = 13\n3 Focus on multiplication by3\n4\nWe can get rid of 3\n4\nby dividing both sides by 3\n4\n. Dividing by a fraction is the\nsame as multiplying by the reciprocal, so we will multiply bo th sides by 4\n3\n. \n( 4\n3\n) 3\n4\nx = 13\n3\n( 4\n3\n)\nMultiply by reciprocal\nx = 52\n9 Our solution! \nWhile this process does help us arrive at the correct solutio n, the fractions can\nmake the process quite diﬃcult. This is why we have an alterna te method for\ndealing with fractions - clearing fractions. Clearing frac tions is nice as it gets rid\nof the fractions for the majority of the problem. We can easil y clear the fractions\n43\nby ﬁnding the LCD and multiplying each term by the LCD. This is shown in the\nnext example, the same problem as our ﬁrst example, but this t ime we will solve\nby clearing fractions. \nExample 70. \n3\n4\nx − 7\n2 \n= 5 \n6\nLCD = 12, multiply each term by 12\n( 12 ) 3\n4 x − ( 12 ) 7\n2 = ( 12 ) 5 \n6 Reduce each 12 with denominators\n(3) 3x − (6) 7 = (2) 5 Multiply out each term\n9x − 42 = 10 Focus on subtraction by 42\n+ 42 + 42 Add 42 to both sides\n9x = 52 Focus on multiplication by 9\n9 9 Divide both sides by9\nx = 52\n9 Our Solution\nThe next example illustrates this as well. Notice the 2 isn’t a fraction in the ori-\ngional equation, but to solve it we put the 2 over 1 to make it a fraction. \nExample 71. \n2 \n3\nx − 2 = 3\n2 \nx + 1 \n6\nLCD = 6, multiply each term by6\n(6) 2 \n3 x − (6) 2 \n1 = (6) 3\n2 x + (6) 1 \n6 Reduce 6 with each denominator\n(2) 2 x − (6) 2 = (3) 3x + (1) 1 Multiply out each term\n4x − 12 = 9x + 1 Notice variable on both sides\n− 4x − 4x Subtract 4x from both sides\n− 12 = 5 x + 1 Focus on addition of1 \n− 1 − 1 Subtract 1 from both sides\n− 13 = 5 x Focus on multiplication of5 \n5 5 Divide both sides by5 \n− 13\n5 = x Our Solution\nWe can use this same process if there are parenthesis in the pr oblem. We will ﬁrst\ndistribute the coeﬃcient in front of the parenthesis, then c lear the fractions. This\nis seen in the following example. \n44"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_8"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Solving with Fractions well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain the concept of Solving with Fractions and its application",
            "Solve linear equations with rational coefficients by multiplying by the least common denominator (LCD)",
            "Recognize when to use clearing fractions as an alternative method"
          ],

              "id": "SEC_8_C1",
              "title": "What is Solving with Fractions?",
              "explanation": "Solving with Fractions involves using the concept of least common denominators to clear fractions in linear equations. This process can be used to simplify problems and make them easier to solve.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_9",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.5",
          "title": "Formulas",
          "order": 8,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 47,
              "page_end": 47,
              "text": "1.5\nSolving Linear Equations - Formulas\nObjective: Solve linear formulas for a given variable. \nSolving formulas is much like solving general linear equati ons. The only diﬀerence\nis we will have several varaibles in the problem and we will be attempting to solve\nfor one speciﬁc variable. For example, we may have a formula s uch as A = πr 2 + \nπ rs (formula for surface area of a right circular cone) and we m ay be interested in\nsolving for the varaible s . This means we want to isolate the s so the equation has\ns on one side, and everything else on the other. So a solution mi ght look like s = \nA − πr2 \nπs\n. This second equation gives the same information as the ﬁrst , they are\nalgebraically equivalent, however, one is solved for the ar ea, while the other is\nsolved for s (slant height of the cone). In this section we will discuss ho w we can\nmove from the ﬁrst equation to the second. \nWhen solving formulas for a variable we need to focus on the on e varaible we are\ntrying to solve for, all the others are treated just like numb ers. This is shown in\nthe following example. Two parallel problems are shown, the ﬁrst is a normal one-\nstep equation, the second is a formula that we are solving for x\nExample 74. \n3x = 12 wx = z In both problems, x is multiplied by something\n3\n3 w w To isolate the x we divide by3 or w. \nx = 4 x = z\nw Our Solution\nWe use the same process to solve 3x = 12 for x as we use to solve w x = z for x. \nBecause we are solving for x we treat all the other variables the same way we\nwould treat numbers. Thus, to get rid of the multiplication w e divided by w. This\nsame idea is seen in the following example. \nExample 75. \nm + n = p for n Solving for n, treat all other variables like numbers\n− m − m\nSubtract m from both sides\nn = p − m Our Solution\nAs p and m are not like terms, they cannot be combined. For this reason w e leave\nthe expression as p − m. This same one-step process can be used with grouping\nsymbols. \n47\nExample 76. \na( x − y) = b for a Solving for a, treat ( x − y) like a number\n( x − y) ( x − y) Divide both sides by( x − y) \na = b\nx − y Our Solution\nBecause ( x − y) is in parenthesis, if we are not searching for what is inside t he\nparenthesis, we can keep them together as a group and divide b y that group. \nHowever, if we are searching for what is inside the parenthes is, we will have to\nbreak up the parenthesis by distributing. The following exa mple is the same for-\nmula, but this time we will solve for x. \nExample 77. \na( x − y) = b for x Solving for x, we need to distribute to clear parenthesis\nax − ay = b This is a two − step equation, ay is subtracted from ourx term\n+ ay + a y Add ay to both sides\nax = b + ay The x is multipied bya\na a Divide both sides by a\nx = b + ay\na Our Solution\nBe very careful as we isolate x that we do not try and cancel the a on top and\nbottom of the fraction. This is not allowed if there is any add ing or subtracting in\nthe fraction. There is no reducing possible in this problem, so our ﬁnal reduced\nanswer remains x = b + ay\na . The next example is another two-step problem\nExample 78. \ny = mx + b for m Solving for m, focus on addition ﬁrst\n− b − b Subtract b from both sides\ny − b = mx m is multipied byx. \nx x Divide both sides byx\ny − b\nx = m Our Solution\nIt is important to note that we know we are done with the proble m when the\nvariable we are solving for is isolated or alone on one side of the equation and it\ndoes not appear anywhere on the other side of the equation. \nThe next example is also a two-step equation, it is the proble m we started with at\nthe beginning of the lesson. \n48"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_9"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Formulas well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain the concept of solving linear formulas for a given variable",
            "Solve straightforward problems involving solving linear formulas",
            "Recognize when to use the formula-solving technique in later course work"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_9_C1",
              "title": "Understanding Linear Formulas",
              "explanation": "A linear formula is an equation that can be written in the form ax + by = c, where a, b, and c are constants. Solving for a variable in a linear formula involves isolating that variable on one side of the equation.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 47"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_10",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.6",
          "title": "Absolute Value Equations",
          "order": 9,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 52,
              "page_end": 52,
              "text": "1.6\nSolving Linear Equations - Absolute Value\nObjective: Solve linear absolute value equations. \nWhen solving equations with absolute value we can end up with more than one\npossible answer. This is because what is in the absolute valu e can be either nega-\ntive or positive and we must account for both possibilities w hen solving equations. \nThis is illustrated in the following example. \nExample 84. \n| x| = 7 Absolute value can be positive or negative\nx = 7 or x = − 7 Our Solution\nNotice that we have considered two possibilities, both the p ositive and negative. \nEither way, the absolute value of our number will be positive 7. \nWorld View Note: The ﬁrst set of rules for working with negatives came from\n7th century India. However, in 1758, almost a thousand years later, British math-\nematician Francis Maseres claimed that negatives “Darken t he very whole doc-\ntrines of the equations and make dark of the things which are i n their nature\nexcessively obvious and simple.”\nWhen we have absolute values in our problem it is important to ﬁrst isolate the\nabsolute value, then remove the absolute value by consideri ng both the positive\nand negative solutions. Notice in the next two examples, all the numbers outside\nof the absolute value are moved to the other side ﬁrst before w e remove the abso-\nlute value bars and consider both positive and negative solu tions. \nExample 85. \n5 + | x| = 8 Notice absolute value is not alone\n− 5 − 5 \nSubtract 5 from both sides\n| x| = 3 Absolute value can be positive or negative\nx = 3 or x = − 3 Our Solution\nExample 86. \n− 4| x| = − 20 Notice absolute value is not alone\n− 4 − 4 Divide both sides by− 4\n52\n| x| = 5 Absolute value can be positive or negative\nx = 5 or x = − 5 Our Solution\nNotice we never combine what is inside the absolute value wit h what is outside\nthe absolute value. This is very important as it will often ch ange the ﬁnal result\nto an incorrect solution. The next example requires two step s to isolate the abso-\nlute value. The idea is the same as a two-step equation, add or subtract, then\nmultiply or divide. \nExample 87. \n5 | x| − 4 = 26 Notice the absolute value is not alone\n+ 4 + 4\nAdd 4 to both sides\n5 | x| = 30 Absolute value still not alone\n5 5 Divide both sides by5 \n| x| = 6 Absolute value can be positive or negative\nx = 6 or x = − 6 Our Solution\nAgain we see the same process, get the absolute value alone ﬁr st, then consider\nthe positive and negative solutions. Often the absolute val ue will have more than\njust a variable in it. In this case we will have to solve the res ulting equations\nwhen we consider the positive and negative possibilities. T his is shown in the next\nexample. \nExample 88. \n| 2 x − 1 | = 7 Absolute value can be positive or negative\n2 x − 1 = 7 or 2 x − 1 = − 7 Two equations to solve\nNow notice we have two equations to solve, each equation will give us a diﬀerent\nsolution. Both equations solve like any other two-step equa tion. \n2 x − 1 = 7\n+ 1 + 1 \n2 x = 8\n2 2 \nx = 4\nor\n2 x − 1 = − 7\n+ 1 + 1 \n2 x = − 6\n2 2 \nx = − 3\n53"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_10"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Absolute Value Equations well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Understand the concept of absolute value equations and their importance in solving linear equations",
            "Explain how to solve straightforward absolute value problems, considering both positive and negative possibilities",
            "Recognize when an absolute value equation is applicable in a problem"
          ],

              "title": "What are Absolute Value Equations?",
              "explanation": "Absolute value equations involve solving for the value inside the absolute value bars. When we have an absolute value equation, we need to consider both the positive and negative possibilities of what's inside the bars.",

                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 52"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_11",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.7",
          "title": "Variation",
          "order": 10,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 57,
              "page_end": 57,
              "text": "1.7\nSolving Linear Equations - Variation\nObjective: Solve variation problems by creating variation equations and\nﬁnding the variation constant. \nOne application of solving linear equations is variation. O ften diﬀerent events are\nrelated by what is called the constant of variation. For exam ple, the time it takes\nto travel a certain distance is related to how fast you are tra veling. The faster you\ntravel, the less time it take to get there. This is one type of v ariation problem, we\nwill look at three types of variation here. Variation proble ms have two or three\nvariables and a constant in them. The constant, usually note d with a k, describes\nthe relationship and does not change as the other variables i n the problem change. \nThere are two ways to set up a variation problem, the ﬁrst solv es for one of the\nvariables, a second method is to solve for the constant. Here we will use the\nsecond method. \nThe greek letter pi ( π ) is used to represent the ratio of the circumference of a\ncircle to its diameter. \nWorld View Note: In the 5th centure, Chinese mathematician Zu Chongzhi cal-\nculated the value of π to seven decimal places (3.1415926). This was the most\naccurate value of π for the next 1000 years! \nIf you take any circle and divide the circumference of the cir cle by the diameter\nyou will always get the same value, about 3.14159... If you ha ve a bigger circum-\nference you will also have a bigger diameter. This relations hip is called direct\nvariation or directly proportional . If we see this phrase in the problem we\nknow to divide to ﬁnd the constant of variation. \nExample 92. \nm is varies directly asn ′′Directly ′′ tells us to divide\nm\nn\n= k Our formula for the relationship\nIn kickboxing, one will ﬁnd that the longer the board, the eas ier it is to break. If\nyou multiply the force required to break a board by the length of the board you\nwill also get a constant. Here, we are multiplying the variab les, which means as\none variable increases, the other variable decreases. This relationship is called\nindirect variation or inversly proportional . If we see this phrase in the\nproblem we know to multiply to ﬁnd the constant of variation.\nExample 93. \ny is inversely proportional toz ′′Inversely′′ tells us to multiply\nyz = k Our formula for the relationship\n57\nThe formula for the area of a triangle has three variables in i t. If we divide the\narea by the base times the height we will also get a constant, 1 \n2 \n. This relationship\nis called joint variation or jointly proportional . If we see this phrase in the\nproblem we know to divide the ﬁrst variable by the product of t he other two to\nﬁnd the constant of variation. \nExample 94. \nA varies jointly asx and y ′′Jointly′′ tells us to divide by the product\nA\nxy\n= k Our formula for the relationship\nOnce we have our formula for the relationship in a variation p roblem, we use\ngiven or known information to calculate the constant of vari ation. This is shown\nfor each type of variation in the next three examples. \nExample 95. \nw is directly proportional to y and w = 50 when y = 5 \nw\ny\n= k ′′directly ′′ tells us to divide\n( 50) \n(5) = k Substitute known values\n10 = k Evaluate to ﬁnd our constant\nExample 96. \nc varies indirectly as d and c = 4.5 when d = 6\ncd = k ′′indirectly ′′ tells us to multiply\n( 4.5)(6) = k Substitute known values\n27 = k Evaluate to ﬁnd our constant\nExample 97. \nx is jointly proportional to y and z and x = 48 when y = 2 and z = 4\nx\nyz = k ′′Jointly′′ tells us to divide by the product\n( 48) \n(2)(4) = k Substitute known values\n6 = k Evaluate to ﬁnd our constant\n58"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_11"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Variation well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

              "id": "SEC_11_C1",
              "title": "What is Variation?",
              "explanation": "Variation is a mathematical relationship between two or more variables that describes how they are related to each other. It's like finding the secret code that links different events together. In variation problems, we use constants to describe this relationship.",

          "id": "SEC_12",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.8",
          "title": "Application: Number/Geometry",
          "order": 11,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 64,
              "page_end": 64,
              "text": "1.8\nLinear Equations - Number and Geometry\nObjective: Solve number and geometry problems by creating a nd\nsolving a linear equation. \nWord problems can be tricky. Often it takes a bit of practice t o convert the\nEnglish sentence into a mathematical sentence. This is what we will focus on here\nwith some basic number problems, geometry problems, and par ts problems. \nA few important phrases are described below that can give us c lues for how to set\nup a problem. \n• A number (or unknown, a value, etc) often becomes our variable\n• Is (or other forms of is: was, will be, are, etc) often represent s equals (=) \nx is 5 becomes x = 5 \n• More than often represents addition and is usually built backwards, \nwriting the second part plus the ﬁrst\nThree more than a number becomes x + 3\n• Less than often represents subtraction and is usually built backward s as\nwell, writing the second part minus the ﬁrst\nFour less than a number becomes x − 4\nUsing these key phrases we can take a number problem and set up and equation\nand solve. \nExample 102. \nIf 28 less than ﬁve times a certain number is 232. What is the nu mber? \n5 x − 28 Subtraction is built backwards , multiply the unknown by5 \n5 x − 28 = 232 Is translates to equals\n+ 28 + 28\nAdd 28 to both sides\n5 x = 260 The variable is multiplied by 5 \n5 5 Divide both sides by5 \nx = 52 The number is 52 . \nThis same idea can be extended to a more involved problem as sh own in the next\nexample. \nExample 103. \n64\nFifteen more than three times a number is the same as ten less t han six times the\nnumber. What is the number\n3x + 15 First , addition is built backwards\n6x − 10 Then , subtraction is also built backwards\n3x + 15 = 6x − 10 Is between the parts tells us they must be equal\n− 3x − 3x Subtract 3x so variable is all on one side\n15 = 3x − 10 Now we have a two − step equation\n+ 10 + 10 Add 10 to both sides\n25 = 3x The variable is multiplied by3\n3 3 Divide both sides by3\n25\n3 = x Our number is 25\n3\nAnother type of number problem involves consecutive number s. Consecutive\nnumbers are numbers that come one after the other, such as 3, 4, 5. If we are\nlooking for several consecutive numbers it is important to ﬁ rst identify what they\nlook like with variables before we set up the equation. This i s shown in the fol-\nlowing example. \nExample 104. \nThe sum of three consecutive integers is 93. What are the inte gers? \nFirst x Make the ﬁrst numberx\nSecond x + 1 To get the next number we go up one or+ 1 \nThird x + 2 Add another 1(2 total) to get the third\nF + S + T = 93 First ( F ) plus Second ( S) plus Third ( T ) equals 93\n( x) + ( x + 1) + ( x + 2) = 93 Replace F with x, S with x + 1 , and T with x + 2 \nx + x + 1 + x + 2 = 93 Here the parenthesis aren ′t needed. \n3x + 3 = 93 Combine like terms x + x + x and 2 + 1 \n− 3 − 3\nAdd 3 to both sides\n3x = 90 The variable is multiplied by 3\n3 3 Divide both sides by3\nx = 30 Our solution for x\nFirst 30 Replace x in our origional list with 30\nSecond ( 30) + 1 = 31 The numbers are 30 , 31 , and 32\nThird ( 30) + 2 = 32\nSometimes we will work consective even or odd integers, rath er than just consecu-\ntive integers. When we had consecutive integers, we only had to add 1 to get to\nthe next number so we had x, x + 1 , and x + 2 for our ﬁrst, second, and third\nnumber respectively. With even or odd numbers they are space d apart by two. So\nif we want three consecutive even numbers, if the ﬁrst is x, the next number\nwould be x + 2 , then ﬁnally add two more to get the third, x + 4 . The same is\n65"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_13",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.9",
          "title": "Application: Age",
          "order": 12,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 72,
              "page_end": 72,
              "text": "1.9\nSolving Linear Equations - Age Problems\nObjective: Solve age problems by creating and solving a line ar equa-\ntion. \nAn application of linear equations is what are called age pro blems. When we are\nsolving age problems we generally will be comparing the age o f two people both\nnow and in the future (or past). Using the clues given in the pr oblem we will be\nworking to ﬁnd their current age. There can be a lot of informa tion in these prob-\nlems and we can easily get lost in all the information. To help us organize and\nsolve our problem we will ﬁll out a three by three table for eac h problem. An\nexample of the basic structure of the table is below\nAge Now Change\nPerson 1 \nPerson 2\nTable 6. Structure of Age Table\nNormally where we see “Person 1” and “Person 2” we will use the name of the\nperson we are talking about. We will use this table to set up th e following\nexample. \nExample 110. \nAdam is 20 years younger than Brian. In two years Brian will be twice as old as\nAdam. How old are they now? \nAge Now + 2 \nAdam\nBrian\nWe use Adam and Brian for our persons\nWe use+ 2 for change because the second phrase\nis two years in the future\nAge Now + 2 \nAdam x − 20\nBrain x\nConsider the ′′Now′′ part, Adam is 20 years\nyouger than Brian. We are given information about\nAdam, not Brian. So Brian is x now. To show Adam\nis 20 years younger we subtract 20, Adam is x − 20. \nAge Now + 2 \nAdam x − 20 x − 20 + 2 \nBrian x x + 2 \nNow the + 2 column is ﬁlled in. This is done by adding\n2 to both Adam′s and Brian′s now column as shown\nin the table. \nAge Now + 2 \nAdam x − 20 x − 18\nBrian x x + 2 \nCombine like terms in Adam′s future age: − 20 + 2 \nThis table is now ﬁlled out and we are ready to try\nand solve. \n72\nB = 2 A\nOur equation comes from the future statement: \nBrian will be twice as old as Adam. This means\nthe younger, Adam, needs to be multiplied by2 . \n( x + 2) = 2( x − 18) \nReplace B and A with the information in their future\ncells, Adam ( A) is replaced with x − 18 and Brian ( B) \nis replaced with ( x + 2) This is the equation to solve! \nx + 2 = 2 x − 36 Distribute through parenthesis\n− x − x Subtract x from both sides to get variable on one side\n2 = x − 36 Need to clear the− 36\n+ 36 + 36 Add 36 to both sides\n38 = x Our solution for x\nAge now\nAdam 38 − 20 = 18\nBrian 38\nThe ﬁrst column will help us answer the question. \nReplace the x′s with 38 and simplify. \nAdam is 18 and Brian is 38\nSolving age problems can be summarized in the following ﬁve s teps. These ﬁve\nsteps are guidelines to help organize the problem we are tryi ng to solve. \n1. Fill in the now column. The person we know nothing about is x. \n2. Fill in the future/past collumn by adding/subtracting th e change to the\nnow column. \n3. Make an equation for the relationship in the future. This i s independent of\nthe table. \n4. Replace variables in equation with information in future cells of table\n5. Solve the equation for x, use the solution to answer the question\nThese ﬁve steps can be seen illustrated in the following exam ple. \nExample 111. \nCarmen is 12 years older than David. Five years ago the sum of t heir ages was 28. \nHow old are they now? \nAge Now − 5 \nCarmen\nDavid\nFive years ago is− 5 in the change column. \nAge Now − 5 \nCarmen x + 12\nDavid x\nCarmen is 12 years older than David. We don′t\nknow about David so he isx, Carmen then is x + 12\nAge Now − 5 \nCarmen x + 12 x + 12 − 5 \nDavid x x − 5 \nSubtract 5 from now column to get the change\n73"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_14",
          "chapter_id": "CH_2",
          "number": "1.10",
          "title": "Application: Distance",
          "order": 13,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 79,
              "page_end": 79,
              "text": "1.10\nSolving Linear Equations - Distance, Rate and Time\nObjective: Solve distance problems by creating and solving a linear\nequation. \nAn application of linear equations can be found in distance p roblems. When\nsolving distance problems we will use the relationship rt = d or rate (speed) times\ntime equals distance. For example, if a person were to travel 30 mph for 4 hours. \nTo ﬁnd the total distance we would multiply rate times time or ( 30)(4) = 120. \nThis person travel a distance of 120 miles. The problems we wi ll be solving here\nwill be a few more steps than described above. So to keep the in formation in the\nproblem organized we will use a table. An example of the basic structure of the\ntable is blow: \nRate Time Distance\nPerson 1 \nPerson 2\nT able 7. Structure of Distance Problem\nThe third column, distance, will always be ﬁlled in by multip lying the rate and\ntime columns together. If we are given a total distance of bot h persons or trips we\nwill put this information below the distance column. We will now use this table to\nset up and solve the following example\n79\nExample 114. \nTwo joggers start from opposite ends of an 8 mile course runni ng towards each\nother. One jogger is running at a rate of 4 mph, and the other is running at a\nrate of 6 mph. After how long will the joggers meet? \nRate Time Distance\nJogger 1 \nJogger 2 \nThe basic table for the joggers, one and two\nRate Time Distance\nJogger 1 4\nJogger 2 6 \nWe are given the rates for each jogger. \nThese are added to the table\nRate Time Distance\nJogger 1 4 t\nJogger 2 6 t\nWe only know they both start and end at the\nsame time. We use the variablet for both times\nRate Time Distance\nJogger 1 4 t 4t\nJogger 2 6 t 6 t\nThe distance column is ﬁlled in by multiplying\nrate by time\n8 We havetotal distance, 8 miles, under distance\n4t + 6t = 8 The distance column gives equation by adding\n10t = 8 Combine like terms, 4t + 6t\n10\n10 Divide both sides by 10\nt = 4\n5 \nOur solution for t, 4\n5 \nhour ( 48 minutes) \nAs the example illustrates, once the table is ﬁlled in, the eq uation to solve is very\neasy to ﬁnd. This same process can be seen in the following exa mple\nExample 115. \nBob and Fred start from the same point and walk in opposite dir ections. Bob\nwalks 2 miles per hour faster than Fred. After 3 hours they are 30 miles apart. \nHow fast did each walk? \nRate Time Distance\nBob 3\nFred 3\nThe basic table with given times ﬁlled in\nBoth traveled3 hours\n80"
            }
          ],

    {
      "id": "CH_3",
      "number": "2",
      "title": "Graphing",
      "order": 2,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_15",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.1",
          "title": "Points and Lines",
          "order": 14,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 89,
              "page_end": 89,
              "text": "2.1 \nGraphing - Points and Lines\nObjective: Graph points and lines using xy coordinates. \nOften, to get an idea of the behavior of an equation we will mak e a picture that\nrepresents the solutions to the equations. A graph is simply a picture of the solu-\ntions to an equation. Before we spend much time on making a vis ual representa-\ntion of an equation, we ﬁrst have to understand the basis of gr aphing. Following\nis an example of what is called the coordinate plane. \n-4 -3 -2 -1 1 2 3\n-2\n-1 \n1 \n2\nThe plane is divided into four sections\nby a horizontal number line ( x-axis) \nand a vertical number line ( y-axis). \nWhere the two lines meet in the center\nis called the origin. This center origin\nis where x = 0 and y = 0 . As we move\nto the right the numbers count up\nfrom zero, representing x = 1 , 2 , 3\n/ . \nTo the left the numbers count down from zero, representing x = − 1 , − 2 , − 3 / . \nSimilarly, as we move up the number count up from zero, y = 1 , 2 , 3 / . , and as we\nmove down count down from zero, y = − 1 , − 2 , − 3. We can put dots on the\ngraph which we will call points. Each point has an “address” t hat deﬁnes its loca-\ntion. The ﬁrst number will be the value on the x − axis or horizontal number line. \nThis is the distance the point moves left/right from the orig in. The second\nnumber will represent the value on the y − axis or vertical number line. This is\nthe distance the point moves up/down from the origin. The poi nts are given as an\nordered pair ( x, y ) . \nWorld View Note: Locations on the globe are given in the same manner, each\nnumber is a distance from a central point, the origin which is where the prime\nmeridian and the equator. This “origin is just oﬀ the western coast of Africa. \nThe following example ﬁnds the address or coordinate pair fo r each of several\npoints on the coordinate plane. \nExample 119. \nGive the coordinates of each point. \nA\nB\nC\n89\nTracing from the origin, point A is\nright 1, up 4. This becomes A (1 , 4) . \nPoint B is left 5, up 3. Left is back-\nwards or negative so we have B ( − 5 , \n3) . C is straight down 2 units. There\nis no left or right. This means we go\nright zero so the point is C (0 , − 2) . \nA(1 , 4) , B ( − 5 , 3) , C (0 , − 2) Our Solution\nJust as we can give the coordinates for a set of points, we can t ake a set of points\nand plot them on the plane. \nExample 120. \nGraph the points A(3, 2) , B ( − 2 , 1) , C (3, − 4) , D ( − 2 , − 3) , E ( − 3, 0) , F (0 , 2) , \nG(0 , 0) \nRight 3\nUp 2\nA\nLeft 2\nUp 1 \nB\nThe ﬁrst point, A is at (3, 2) this\nmeans x = 3 (right 3) and y = 2 (up 2). \nFollowing these instructions, starting\nfrom the origin, we get our point. \nThe second point, B ( − 2 , 1) , is left 2\n(negative moves backwards), up 1. \nThis is also illustrated on the graph. \nA\nB\nD\nC\nRight 3Left 2\nDown 3 Down 4\nThe third point, C (3, − 4) is right 3, \ndown 4 (negative moves backwards). \nThe fourth point, D ( − 2 , − 3) is left\n2, down 3 (both negative, both move\nbackwards) \nThe last three points have zeros in them. We still treat these points just like the\nother points. If there is a zero there is just no movement. \nUp 2\nLeft 3\nD\nA\nC\nB F\nGE\nNext is E ( − 3, 0) . This is left 3 (nega-\ntive is backwards), and up zero, right\non the x − axis. \nThen is F (0 , 2) . This is right zero, \nand up two, right on the y − axis. \nFinally is G (0 , 0) . This point has no\nmovement. Thus the point is right on\nthe origin. \n90"
            }
          ],

            "Be able to explain the coordinate plane and how it is used to locate points.",
            "Know how to plot points on the coordinate plane using xy coordinates."

              "id": "SEC_15_C1",
              "title": "Points and Lines",
              "explanation": "In graphing, a point is a location on the coordinate plane with an address or coordinate pair (x, y). A line is a set of points that extend infinitely in two directions. Understanding points and lines is crucial for visualizing the solutions to equations.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 89"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_16",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.2",
          "title": "Slope",
          "order": 15,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 95,
              "page_end": 95,
              "text": "2.2\nGraphing - Slope\nObjective: Find the slope of a line given a graph or two points . \nAs we graph lines, we will want to be able to identify diﬀerent properties of the\nlines we graph. One of the most important properties of a line is its slope. Slope\nis a measure of steepness. A line with a large slope, such as 25 , is very steep. A\nline with a small slope, such as 1 \n10 is very ﬂat. We will also use slope to describe\nthe direction of the line. A line that goes up from left to righ t will have a positive\nslope and a line that goes down from left to right will have a ne gative slope. \nAs we measure steepness we are interested in how fast the line rises compared to\nhow far the line runs. For this reason we will describe slope a s the fraction rise\nrun\n. \nRise would be a vertical change, or a change in the y-values. Run would be a hor-\nizontal change, or a change in the x-values. So another way to describe slope\nwould be the fraction change iny\nchange inx . It turns out that if we have a graph we can draw\nvertical and horiztonal lines from one point to another to ma ke what is called a\nslope triangle. The sides of the slope triangle give us our sl ope. The following\nexamples show graphs that we ﬁnd the slope of using this idea.\nExample 123. \nRun 6\nRise − 4\nTo ﬁnd the slope of this line we will\nconsider the rise, or verticle change\nand the run or horizontal change. \nDrawing these lines in makes a slope\ntriangle that we can use to count from\none point to the next the graph goes\ndown 4, right 6. This is rise − 4, run\n6. As a fraction it would be, − 4\n6\n. \nReduce the fraction to get − 2 \n3\n. \n− 2 \n3\nOur Solution\nWorld View Note: When French mathematicians Rene Descartes and Pierre de\nFermat ﬁrst developed the coordinate plane and the idea of gr aphing lines (and\nother functions) the y-axis was not a verticle line! \nExample 124. \n95\nRise 6\nRun 3 To ﬁnd the slope of this line, the rise\nis up 6, the run is right 3. Our slope is\nthen written as a fraction, rise\nrun\nor 6\n3\n. \nThis fraction reduces to 2. This will\nbe our slope. \n2 Our Solution\nThere are two special lines that have unique slopes that we ne ed to be aware of. \nThey are illustrated in the following example. \nExample 125. \nIn this graph there is no rise, but the\nrun is 3 units. This slope becomes\n0\n3\n= 0 . This line , \nand all horizontal lines havea zero slope. \nThis line has a rise of 5, but no run. \nThe slope becomes 5\n0\n= \nundeﬁned. This line , \nand all vertical lines, have no slope. \nAs you can see there is a big diﬀerence between having a zero sl ope and having no\nslope or undeﬁned slope. Remember, slope is a measure of stee pness. The ﬁrst\nslope is not steep at all, in fact it is ﬂat. Therefore it has a z ero slope. The\nsecond slope can’t get any steeper. It is so steep that there i s no number large\nenough to express how steep it is. This is an undeﬁned slope. \nWe can ﬁnd the slope of a line through two points without seein g the points on a\ngraph. We can do this using a slope formula. If the rise is the c hange in y values, \nwe can calculate this by subtracting the y values of a point. Similarly, if run is a\nchange in the x values, we can calculate this by subtracting the x values of a\npoint. In this way we get the following equation for slope. \nThe slope of a line through ( x 1 , y 1 ) and ( x 2 , y 2 ) is y2 − y1 \nx 2 − x 1 \n96"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_16"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Slope well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain the concept of slope and its measurement",
            "Identify and calculate the slope of a line given a graph or two points",
            "Recognize the difference between zero, undefined, and negative slopes"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_16_C1",
              "title": "What is Slope?",
              "explanation": "Slope measures the steepness of a line. It's a fraction that compares how fast a line rises (vertically) to how far it runs (horizontally). A larger slope value means a steeper line, while a smaller slope value indicates a flatter line.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 95"
              ],
              "prerequisites": [
                "SEC_14_C1",
                "SEC_15_C1"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_17",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.3",
          "title": "Slope-Intercept Form",
          "order": 16,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 102,
              "page_end": 102,
              "text": "2.3\nGraphing - Slope-Intercept Form\nObjective: Give the equation of a line with a known slope and y -inter-\ncept. \nWhen graphing a line we found one method we could use is to make a table of\nvalues. However, if we can identify some properties of the li ne, we may be able to\nmake a graph much quicker and easier. One such method is ﬁndin g the slope and\nthe y-intercept of the equation. The slope can be represente d by m and the y-\nintercept, where it crosses the axis and x = 0 , can be represented by (0 , b ) where b\nis the value where the graph crosses the vertical y-axis. Any other point on the\nline can be represented by ( x, y ) . Using this information we will look at the slope\nformula and solve the formula for y. \nExample 132. \nm, (0 , b ) , ( x, y ) Using the slope formula gives: \ny − b\nx − 0 \n= m Simplify\ny − b\nx = m Multiply both sides by x\ny − b = mx Add b to both sides\n+ b + b\ny = mx + b Our Solution\nThis equation, y = m x + b can be thought of as the equation of any line that as a\nslope of m and a y-intercept of b. This formula is known as the slope-intercept\nequation. \nSlope − Intercept Equation: y = m x + b\nIf we know the slope and the y-intercept we can easily ﬁnd the e quation that rep-\nresents the line. \nExample 133. \nSlope = 3\n4\n, y − intercept = − 3 Use the slope − intercept equation\ny = mx + b m is the slope, b is the y − intercept\ny = 3\n4\nx − 3 Our Solution\nWe can also ﬁnd the equation by looking at a graph and ﬁnding th e slope and y-\nintercept. \nExample 134. \n102\nIdentify the point where the graph\ncrosses the y-axis (0,3). This means\nthe y-intercept is 3. \nIdenﬁty one other point and draw a\nslope triangle to ﬁnd the slope. The\nslope is − 2 \n3\ny = mx + b Slope-intercept equation\ny = − 2 \n3\nx + 3 Our Solution\nWe can also move the opposite direction, using the equation i dentify the slope\nand y-intercept and graph the equation from this informatio n. However, it will be\nimportant for the equation to ﬁrst be in slope intercept form . If it is not, we will\nhave to solve it for y so we can identify the slope and the y-intercept. \nExample 135. \nWrite in slope− intercept form: 2 x − 4y = 6 Solve for y\n− 2 x − 2 x Subtract 2 x from both sides\n− 4y = − 2 x + 6 Put x term ﬁrst\n− 4 − 4 − 4 Divide each term by− 4\ny = 1 \n2 \nx − 3\n2 \nOur Solution\nOnce we have an equation in slope-intercept form we can graph it by ﬁrst plotting\nthe y-intercept, then using the slope, ﬁnd a second point and connecting the dots. \nExample 136. \nGraph y = 1 \n2 \nx − 4 Recall the slope − intercept formula\ny = mx + b Idenﬁty the slope, m, and the y − intercept, b\nm = 1 \n2 \n, b = − 4 Make the graph\nStarting with a point at the y-inter-\ncept of − 4, \nThen use the slope rise\nrun\n, so we will rise\n1 unit and run 2 units to ﬁnd the next\npoint. \nOnce we have both points, connect the\ndots to get our graph. \nWorld View Note: Before our current system of graphing, French Mathemati-\ncian Nicole Oresme, in 1323 sugggested graphing lines that w ould look more like a\n103"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_17"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Slope-Intercept Form well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Solve equations in Slope-Intercept Form",
            "Graph lines using Slope-Intercept Form",
            "Recognize when to use Slope-Intercept Form"
          ],
          "concepts": [

              "id": "SEC_17_C1",
              "title": "Slope-Intercept Form",
              "explanation": "The equation of a line can be written in the form y = mx + b, where m is the slope and b is the y-intercept. This form allows for easy graphing and understanding of the line's properties.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_18",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.4",
          "title": "Point-Slope Form",
          "order": 17,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 107,
              "page_end": 107,
              "text": "2.4\nGraphing - Point-Slope Form\nObjective: Give the equation of a line with a known slope and p oint. \nThe slope-intercept form has the advantage of being simple t o remember and use, \nhowever, it has one major disadvantage: we must know the y-in tercept in order to\nuse it! Generally we do not know the y-intercept, we only know one or more\npoints (that are not the y-intercept). In these cases we can’ t use the slope inter-\ncept equation, so we will use a diﬀerent more ﬂexible formula . If we let the slope\nof an equation be m, and a speciﬁc point on the line be ( x1 , y 1 ) , and any other\npoint on the line be ( x, y ) . We can use the slope formula to make a second equa-\ntion. \nExample 139. \nm, ( x1 , y 1 ) , ( x, y ) Recall slope formula\ny2 − y1 \nx2 − x1 \n= m Plug in values\ny − y1 \nx − x1 \n= m Multiply both sides by( x − x1 ) \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Our Solution\nIf we know the slope, m of an equation and any point on the line ( x1 , y 1 ) we can\neasily plug these values into the equation above which will b e called the point-\nslope formula. \nPoint − Slope Formula: y − y1 = m( x − x 1 ) \nExample 140. \nWrite the equation of the line through the point (3, − 4) with a slope of 3\n5\n. \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Plug values into point− slope formula\ny − ( − 4) = 3\n5 \n( x − 3) Simplify signs\ny + 4 = 3\n5 \n( x − 3) Our Solution\nOften, we will prefer ﬁnal answers be written in slope interc ept form. If the direc-\n107\ntions ask for the answer in slope-intercept form we will simp ly distribute the\nslope, then solve for y. \nExample 141. \nWrite the equation of the line through the point ( − 6, 2) with a slope of − 2 \n3\nin\nslope-intercept form. \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Plug values into point− slope formula\ny − 2 = − 2 \n3\n( x − ( − 6)) Simplify signs\ny − 2 = − 2 \n3\n( x + 6) Distribute slope\ny − 2 = − 2 \n3\nx − 4 Solve for y\n+ 2 + 2 \ny = − 2 \n3\nx − 2 Our Solution\nAn important thing to observe about the point slope formula i s that the operation\nbetween the x’s and y’s is subtraction. This means when you simplify the signs\nyou will have the opposite of the numbers in the point. We need to be very\ncareful with signs as we use the point-slope formula. \nIn order to ﬁnd the equation of a line we will always need to kno w the slope. If\nwe don’t know the slope to begin with we will have to do some wor k to ﬁnd it\nﬁrst before we can get an equation. \nExample 142. \nFind the equation of the line through the points ( − 2 , 5) and (4, − 3) . \nm = y2 − y1 \nx2 − x1 \nFirst we must ﬁnd the slope\nm = − 3 − 5 \n4 − ( − 2) = − 8\n6 = − 4\n3\nPlug values in slope formula and evaluate\ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) With slope and either point, use point − slope formula\ny − 5 = − 4\n3\n( x − ( − 2)) Simplify signs\ny − 5 = − 4\n3\n( x + 2) Our Solution\nExample 143. \n108"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_18"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Point-Slope Form well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain the concept of Point-Slope Form and its advantages",
            "Use the Point-Slope Formula to write an equation of a line with a known slope and point",
            "Apply the Point-Slope Formula to solve problems involving lines"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_18_C1",
              "title": "What is Point-Slope Form?",
              "explanation": "Point-Slope Form is an equation of a line that uses the slope and a point on the line. It's more flexible than Slope-Intercept Form because it doesn't require knowing the y-intercept.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 107"
              ],
