        {
          "id": "SEC_19",
          "chapter_id": "CH_3",
          "number": "2.5",
          "title": "Parallel & Perpendicular Lines",
          "order": 18,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 112,
              "page_end": 112,
              "text": "2.5\nGraphing - Parallel and Perpendicular Lines\nObjective: Identify the equation of a line given a parallel o r perpendic-\nular line. \nThere is an interesting connection between the slope of line s that are parallel and\nthe slope of lines that are perpendicular (meet at a right ang le). This is shown in\nthe following example. \nExample 145. \nThe above graph has two parallel\nlines. The slope of the top line is\ndown 2, run 3, or − 2 \n3\n. The slope of\nthe bottom line is down 2, run 3 as\nwell, or − 2 \n3\n. \nThe above graph has two perpendic-\nular lines. The slope of the ﬂatter line\nis up 2, run 3 or\n2 \n3\n. The slope of the steeper line is down 3, \nrun 2 or − 3\n2 \n. \nWorld View Note: Greek Mathematician Euclid lived around 300 BC and pub-\nlished a book titled, The Elements . In it is the famous parallel postulate which\nmathematicians have tried for years to drop from the list of p ostulates. The\nattempts have failed, yet all the work done has developed new types of geome-\ntries! \nAs the above graphs illustrate, parallel lines have the same slope and perpendic-\nular lines have opposite (one positive, one negative) recip rocal (ﬂipped fraction) \nslopes. We can use these properties to make conclusions abou t parallel and per-\npendicular lines. \nExample 146. \nFind the slope of a line parallel to 5 y − 2 x = 7. \n5 y − 2 x = 7 To ﬁnd the slope we will put equation in slope− intercept form\n+ 2 x + 2 x\nAdd 2 x to both sides\n5 y = 2 x + 7 Put x term ﬁrst\n5 5 5 Divide each term by5 \ny = 2 \n5 \nx + 7\n5 \nThe slope is the coeﬃcient of x\n112\nm = 2 \n5 \nSlope of ﬁrst line. Parallel lines have the same slope\nm = 2 \n5 \nOur Solution\nExample 147. \nFind the slope of a line perpendicular to 3x − 4y = 2 \n3x − 4y = 2 To ﬁnd slope we will put equation in slope− intercept form\n− 3x − 3x\nSubtract 3x from both sides\n− 4y = − 3x + 2 Put x term ﬁrst\n− 4 − 4 − 4 Divide each term by− 4\ny = 3\n4\nx − 1 \n2 \nThe slope is the coeﬃcient of x\nm = 3\n4\nSlope of ﬁrst lines. Perpendicular lines have opposite reciprocal slopes\nm = − 4\n3\nOur Solution\nOnce we have a slope, it is possible to ﬁnd the complete equati on of the second\nline if we know one point on the second line. \nExample 148. \nFind the equation of a line through (4, − 5) and parallel to 2 x − 3y = 6. \n2 x − 3y = 6 We ﬁrst need slope of parallel line\n− 2 x − 2 x\nSubtract 2 x from each side\n− 3y = − 2 x + 6 Put x term ﬁrst\n− 3 − 3 − 3 Divide each term by− 3\ny = 2 \n3\nx − 2 Identify the slope, the coeﬃcient of x\nm = 2 \n3\nParallel lines have the same slope\nm = 2 \n3\nWe will use this slope and our point(4, − 5) \ny − y1 = m( x − x1 ) Plug this information into point slope formula\ny − ( − 5) = 2 \n3\n( x − 4) Simplify signs\ny + 5 = 2 \n3\n( x − 4) Our Solution\n113"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_19"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Parallel & Perpendicular Lines well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain the concepts of parallel and perpendicular lines",
            "Identify the slope of a line given its equation",
            "Find the equation of a line that is parallel or perpendicular to another line"
          ],
          "concepts": [

              "id": "SEC_19_C1",
              "title": "Parallel Lines",
              "explanation": "Two lines are parallel if they have the same slope. This means that if you know the slope of one line, you can immediately tell whether any other line has the same slope.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 112"
              ],

    {
      "id": "CH_4",
      "number": "3",
      "title": "Inequalities",
      "order": 3,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_20",
          "chapter_id": "CH_4",
          "number": "3.1",
          "title": "Solve and Graph Inequalities",
          "order": 19,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 118,
              "page_end": 118,
              "text": "3.1 \nInequalities - Solve and Graph Inequalities\nObjective: Solve, graph, and give interval notation for the solution to\nlinear inequalities. \nWhen we have an equation such as x = 4 we have a speciﬁc value for our variable. \nWith inequalities we will give a range of values for our varia ble. To do this we\nwill not use equals, but one of the following symbols: \n> Greater than\n⩾ Greater than or equal to\n< Less than\n⩽ Less than or equal to\nWorld View Note: English mathematician Thomas Harriot ﬁrst used the above\nsymbols in 1631. However, they were not immediately accepte d as symbols such\nas ⊏ and ⊐ were already coined by another English mathematician, Will iam\nOughtred. \nIf we have an expression such as x < 4, this means our variable can be any number\nsmaller than 4 such as − 2 , 0 , 3, 3.9 or even 3.999999999 as long as it is smaller\n118\nthan 4. If we have an expression such as x ⩾ − 2 , this means our variable can be\nany number greater than or equal to − 2 , such as 5 , 0 , − 1 , − 1.9999, or even − 2 . \nBecause we don’t have one set value for our variable, it is oft en useful to draw a\npicture of the solutions to the inequality on a number line. W e will start from the\nvalue in the problem and bold the lower part of the number line if the variable is\nsmaller than the number, and bold the upper part of the number line if the vari-\nable is larger. The value itself we will mark with brackets, e ither ) or ( for less\nthan or greater than respectively, and ] or [ for less than or e qual to or greater\nthan or equal to respectively. \nOnce the graph is drawn we can quickly convert the graph into w hat is called\ninterval notation. Interval notation gives two numbers, th e ﬁrst is the smallest\nvalue, the second is the largest value. If there is no largest value, we can use ∞ \n(inﬁnity). If there is no smallest value, we can use − ∞ negative inﬁnity. If we\nuse either positive or negative inﬁnity we will always use a c urved bracket for that\nvalue. \nExample 151. \nGraph the inequality and give the interval notation\nx < 2 Start at 2 and shade below\nUse ) for less than\nOur Graph\n( − ∞ , 2) Interval Notation\nExample 152. \nGraph the inequality and give the interval notation\ny ⩾ − 1 Start at − 1 and shade above\nUse [ for greater than or equal\nOur Graph\n[ − 1 , ∞ ) Interval Notation\nWe can also take a graph and ﬁnd the inequality for it. \n119"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_20"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Solve and Graph Inequalities well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Solve linear inequalities using various symbols",
            "Graph inequalities on a number line",
            "Convert graphs to interval notation",
            "Apply Solve and Graph Inequalities accurately to new problems"
          ],

              "title": "Understanding Linear Inequalities",
              "explanation": "Linear inequalities give a range of values for the variable, unlike equations which have a specific value. To solve an inequality, we need to determine the set of values that make the statement true.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 118"
              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_21",
          "chapter_id": "CH_4",
          "number": "3.2",
          "title": "Compound Inequalitites",
          "order": 20,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 124,
              "page_end": 124,
              "text": "3.2\nInequalities - Compound Inequalities\nObjective: Solve, graph and give interval notation to the so lution of\ncompound inequalities. \nSeveral inequalities can be combined together to form what a re called compound\ninequalities. There are three types of compound inequaliti es which we will investi-\ngate in this lesson. \nThe ﬁrst type of a compound inequality is an OR inequality. Fo r this type of\ninequality we want a true statment from either one inequalit y OR the other\ninequality OR both. When we are graphing these type of inequa lities we will\ngraph each individual inequality above the number line, the n move them both\ndown together onto the actual number line for our graph that c ombines them\ntogether. \nWhen we give interval notation for our solution, if there are two diﬀerent parts to\nthe graph we will put a ∪ (union) symbol between two sets of interval notation, \none for each part. \nExample 157. \nSolve each inequality, graph the solution, and give interva l notation of solution\n2 x − 5 > 3 or 4 − x ⩾ 6 Solve each inequality\n+ 5 + 5 \n− 4 − 4 Add or subtract ﬁrst\n2 x > 8 or − x ⩾ 2 Divide\n2 2 − 1 − 1 Dividing by negative ﬂips sign\nx > 4 or x ⩽ − 2 Graph the inequalities separatly above number line\n( − ∞ , − 2] ∪ (4, ∞ ) Interval Notation\nWorld View Note: The symbol for inﬁnity was ﬁrst used by the Romans, \nalthough at the time the number was used for 1000. The greeks a lso used the\nsymbol for 10,000. \nThere are several diﬀerent results that could result from an OR statement. The\ngraphs could be pointing diﬀerent directions, as in the grap h above, or pointing in\nthe same direction as in the graph below on the left, or pointi ng opposite direc-\ntions, but overlapping as in the graph below on the right. Not ice how interval\nnotation works for each of these cases. \n124\nAs the graphs overlap, we take the\nlargest graph for our solution. \nInterval Notation: ( − ∞ , 1) \nWhen the graphs are combined they\ncover the entire number line. \nInterval Notation: ( − ∞ , ∞ ) or R\nThe second type of compound inequality is an AND inequality. AND inequalities\nrequire both statements to be true. If one is false, they both are false. When we\ngraph these inequalities we can follow a similar process, ﬁr st graph both inequali-\nties above the number line, but this time only where they over lap will be drawn\nonto the number line for our ﬁnal graph. When our solution is g iven in interval\nnotation it will be expressed in a manner very similar to sing le inequalities (there\nis a symbol that can be used for AND, the intersection - ∩ , but we will not use it\nhere). \nExample 158. \nSolve each inequality, graph the solution, and express it in terval notation. \n2 x + 8 ⩾ 5 x − 7 and 5 x − 3 > 3x + 1 Move variables to one side\n− 2 x − 2 x\n− 3x − 3x\n8 ⩾ 3x − 7 and 2 x − 3 > 1 Add 7 or 3 to both sides\n+ 7 + 7 + 3 + 3\n15 ⩾ 3x and 2 x > 4 Divide\n3 3 2 2 \n5 ⩾ x and x > 2 Graph, x is smaller ( or equal ) than 5 , \ngreater than 2 \n(2 , 5] Interval Notation\nAgain, as we graph AND inequalities, only the overlapping pa rts of the individual\ngraphs makes it to the ﬁnal number line. As we graph AND inequa lities there are\nalso three diﬀerent types of results we could get. The ﬁrst is shown in the above\n125"
            }
          ],

              ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_22",
          "chapter_id": "CH_4",
          "number": "3.3",
          "title": "Absolute Value Inequalities",
          "order": 21,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 128,
              "page_end": 128,
              "text": "3.3\nInequalities - Absolute Value Inequalities\nObjective: Solve, graph and give interval notation for the s olution to\ninequalities with absolute values. \nWhen an inequality has an absolute value we will have to remov e the absolute\nvalue in order to graph the solution or give interval notatio n. The way we remove\nthe absolute value depends on the direction of the inequalit y symbol. \nConsider | x| < 2 . \nAbsolute value is deﬁned as distance from zero. Another way t o read this\ninequality would be the distance from zero is less than 2. So o n a number line we\nwill shade all points that are less than 2 units away from zero . \nThis graph looks just like the graphs of the three part compou nd inequalities! \nWhen the absolute value is less than a number we will remove the absolute value\nby changing the problem to a three part inequality, with the n egative value on the\nleft and the positive value on the right. So | x| < 2 becomes − 2 < x < 2 , as the\ngraph above illustrates. \nConsider | x| > 2 . \nAbsolute value is deﬁned as distance from zero. Another way t o read this\ninequality would be the distance from zero is greater than 2. So on the number\nline we shade all points that are more than 2 units away from ze ro. \nThis graph looks just like the graphs of the OR compound inequ alities! When the\nabsolute value is greater than a number we will remove the absolute value by\nchanging the problem to an OR inequality, the ﬁrst inequalit y looking just like\nthe problem with no absolute value, the second ﬂipping the in equality symbol and\nchanging the value to a negative. So | x| > 2 becomes x > 2 or x < − 2 , as the graph\nabove illustrates. \nWorld View Note: The phrase “absolute value” comes from German mathemati-\ncian Karl Weierstrass in 1876, though he used the absolute va lue symbol for com-\nplex numbers. The ﬁrst known use of the symbol for integers co mes from a 1939\n128\nedition of a college algebra text! \nFor all absolute value inequalities we can also express our a nswers in interval\nnotation which is done the same way it is done for standard com pound inequali-\nties. \nWe can solve absolute value inequalities much like we solved absolute value equa-\ntions. Our ﬁrst step will be to isolate the absolute value. Ne xt we will remove the\nabsolute value by making a three part inequality if the absol ute value is less than\na number, or making an OR inequality if the absolute value is g reater than a\nnumber. Then we will solve these inequalites. Remember, if w e multiply or divide\nby a negative the inequality symbol will switch directions!\nExample 160. \nSolve, graph, and give interval notation for the solution\n| 4x − 5 | ⩾ 6 Absolute value is greater, use OR\n4x − 5 ⩾ 6 OR 4x − 5 ⩽ − 6 Solve\n+ 5 + 5 \n+ 5 + 5 Add 5 to both sides\n4x ⩾ 11 OR 4x ⩽ − 1 Divide both sides by4\n4 4 4 4\nx ⩾ 11 \n4 OR x ⩽ − 1 \n4\nGraph\n(\n− ∞ , − 1 \n4\n]\n∪ \n[ 11 \n4 , ∞ \n)\nInterval notation\nExample 161. \nSolve, graph, and give interval notation for the solution\n− 4 − 3| x| ⩽ − 16 Add 4 to both sides\n+ 4 + 4\n− 3| x| ⩽ − 12 Divide both sides by − 3\n− 3 − 3 Dividing by a negative switches the symbol\n| x| ⩾ 4 Absolute value is greater, use OR\n129"
            }

              "id": "SEC_22_C2",
              "title": "Removing Absolute Value",
              "explanation": "To remove the absolute value from an inequality, we need to consider two cases: when the absolute value is less than a number and when it's greater. If the absolute value is less than a number, we create a three-part inequality with the negative value on the left and the positive value on the right. If the absolute value is greater than a number, we create an OR inequality by keeping one of the inequalities as is and flipping the other with the inequality symbol and changing the value to its opposite.",

              "id": "SEC_22_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Introduction to Absolute Value Inequalities",
              "body": "Absolute value inequalities may seem intimidating at first, but they're actually quite straightforward once you understand the concept. The absolute value of a number is its distance from zero on the number line. When we have an inequality involving an absolute value, we need to consider both the positive and negative possibilities of the expression within the absolute value. This is because the absolute value can be thought of as representing the distance between two numbers. By understanding how to solve absolute value inequalities, you'll be able to represent and solve a wide range of mathematical problems involving distances and relationships.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 128"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_22_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Absolute Value Inequality",
              "body": "An absolute value inequality is an expression that involves an absolute value being compared to a specific value using an inequality symbol. The general form of an absolute value inequality is |x| ≤ a, where x is the variable and a is a constant. When solving an absolute value inequality, we need to consider two cases: when the absolute value is less than a number (|x| < a) and when it's greater than a number (|x| > a). In both cases, we can remove the absolute value by creating a three-part inequality or an OR inequality, respectively.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 128"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_22_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solving Absolute Value Inequality Example",
              "body": "Let's solve the absolute value inequality |x| ≤ 4. To do this, we first consider two cases: x ≥ 0 and x < 0. If x ≥ 0, then |x| = x, so we have x ≤ 4. If x < 0, then |x| = -x, so we have -x ≤ 4. Solving for x in both cases gives us the solution set x ∈ [-4, 4].",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 128"
              ]
            },

              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Incorrectly Removing Absolute Value",
              "body": "One common mistake when solving absolute value inequalities is to incorrectly remove the absolute value.           "id": "SEC_23",

          "title": "Graphing",
          "order": 22,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 134,
              "page_end": 134,
              "text": "4.1 \nSystems of Equations - Graphing\nObjective: Solve systems of equations by graphing and ident ifying the\npoint of intersection. \nWe have solved problems like 3x − 4 = 11 by adding 4 to both sides and then\ndividing by 3 (solution is x = 5) . We also have methods to solve equations with\nmore than one variable in them. It turns out that to solve for m ore than one vari-\nable we will need the same number of equations as variables. F or example, to\nsolve for two variables such as x and y we will need two equations. When we have\nseveral equations we are using to solve, we call the equation s a system of equa-\ntions. When solving a system of equations we are looking for a solut ion that\nworks in both equations. This solution is usually given as an ordered pair ( x, y ) . \nThe following example illustrates a solution working in bot h equations\nExample 165. \nShow (2,1) is the solution to the system 3x − y = 5 \nx + y = 3\n(2 , 1) Identify x and y from the orderd pair\nx = 2 , y = 1 Plug these values into each equation\n3(2) − (1) = 5 First equation\n6 − 1 = 5 Evaluate\n5 = 5 True\n(2) + (1) = 3 Second equation, evaluate\n3 = 3 True\nAs we found a true statement for both equations we know (2,1) i s the solution to\nthe system. It is in fact the only combination of numbers that works in both\nequations. In this lesson we will be working to ﬁnd this point given the equations. \nIt seems to follow that if we use points to describe the soluti on, we can use graphs\nto ﬁnd the solutions. \nIf the graph of a line is a picture of all the solutions, we can g raph two lines on\nthe same coordinate plane to see the solutions of both equati ons. We are inter-\n134\nested in the point that is a solution for both lines, this woul d be where the lines\nintersect! If we can ﬁnd the intersection of the lines we have found the solution\nthat works in both equations. \nExample 166. \ny = − 1 \n2 \nx + 3\ny = 3\n4\nx − 2 \nTo graph we identify slopes andy − intercepts\nFirst: m = − 1 \n2 \n, b = 3\nSecond: m = 3\n4\n, b = − 2 \nNow we can graph both lines on the same plane. \n(4,1) \nTo graph each equation, we start at\nthe y-intercept and use the slope rise\nrun\nto get the next point and connect the\ndots. \nRemember a negative slope is down-\nhill! \nFind the intersection point, (4,1) \n(4,1) Our Solution\nOften our equations won’t be in slope-intercept form and we w ill have to solve\nboth equations for y ﬁrst so we can idenﬁty the slope and y-intercept. \nExample 167. \n6x − 3y = − 9\n2 x + 2 y = − 6 Solve each equation fory\n6x − 3y = − 9 2 x + 2 y = − 6\n− 6x − 6x\n− 2 x − 2 x Subtract x terms\n− 3y = − 6x − 9 2 y = − 2 x − 6 Put x terms ﬁrst\n− 3 − 3 − 3 2 2 2 Divide by coeﬃcient of y\ny = 2 x + 3 y = − x − 3 Identify slope and y − intercepts\n135"
            }
          ],

              "body": "Graphing is defined as the process of visualizing and solving systems of equations by identifying the intersection point of two or more lines on a coordinate plane.               "body": "Consider the system of equations 3x - y = 5 and x + y = 3. To graph this system, we first need to identify the slope-intercept form of each equation. Next, we plot each line on the same coordinate plane using the slope-intercept form.         {
          "id": "SEC_24",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",
          "number": "4.2",
          "title": "Substitution",
          "order": 23,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 139,
              "page_end": 139,
              "text": "4.2\nSystems of Equations - Substitution\nObjective: Solve systems of equations using substitution.\nWhen solving a system by graphing has several limitations. F irst, it requires the\ngraph to be perfectly drawn, if the lines are not straight we m ay arrive at the\nwrong answer. Second, graphing is not a great method to use if the answer is\nreally large, over 100 for example, or if the answer is a decim al the that graph will\nnot help us ﬁnd, 3.2134 for example. For these reasons we will rarely use graphing\nto solve our systems. Instead, an algebraic approach will be used. \nThe ﬁrst algebraic approach is called substitution. We will build the concepts of\nsubstitution through several example, then end with a ﬁve-s tep process to solve\nproblems using this method. \nExample 170. \nx = 5 \ny = 2 x − 3 We already knowx = 5 , substitute this into the other equation\ny = 2 (5) − 3 Evaluate, multiply ﬁrst\ny = 10 − 3 Subtract\ny = 7 We now also havey\n(5 , 7) Our Solution\nWhen we know what one variable equals we can plug that value (o r expression) in\nfor the variable in the other equation. It is very important t hat when we substi-\ntute, the substituted value goes in parenthesis. The reason for this is shown in the\nnext example. \nExample 171. \n2 x − 3y = 7\ny = 3x − 7 We knowy = 3x − 7, substitute this into the other equation\n2 x − 3(3x − 7) = 7 Solve this equation, distributing − 3 ﬁrst\n139\n2 x − 9x + 21 = 7 Combine like terms2 x − 9x\n− 7x + 21 = 7 Subtract 21 \n− 21 − 21 \n− 7x = − 14 Divide by − 7\n− 7 − 7\nx = 2 We now have ourx, plug into the y = equation to ﬁnd y\ny = 3(2) − 7 Evaluate, multiply ﬁrst\ny = 6 − 7 Subtract\ny = − 1 We now also havey\n(2 , − 1) Our Solution\nBy using the entire expression 3x − 7 to replace y in the other equation we were\nable to reduce the system to a single linear equation which we can easily solve for\nour ﬁrst variable. However, the lone variable (a variable wi thout a coeﬃcient) is\nnot always alone on one side of the equation. If this happens w e can isolate it by\nsolving for the lone variable. \nExample 172. \n3x + 2 y = 1 \nx − 5 y = 6 Lone variable isx, isolate by adding5 y to both sides. \n+ 5 y + 5 y\nx = 6 + 5 y Substitute this into the untouched equation\n3(6 + 5 y) + 2 y = 1 Solve this equation, distributing 3 ﬁrst\n18 + 15y + 2 y = 1 Combine like terms 15y + 2 y\n18 + 17y = 1 Subtract 18 from both sides\n− 18 − 18\n17y = − 17 Divide both sides by 17\n17 17\ny = − 1 We have oury, plug this into thex = equation to ﬁnd x\nx = 6 + 5 ( − 1) Evaluate, multiply ﬁrst\nx = 6 − 5 Subtract\nx = 1 We now also havex\n(1 , − 1) Our Solution\nThe process in the previous example is how we will solve probl ems using substitu-\n140"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_24"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Substitution well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

            "Explain the concept of substitution in solving systems of equations",
            "Apply substitution to a new problem without copying the source"
          ],
          "concepts": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_24_C1",
              "title": "Substitution Method",
              "explanation": "Substitution is an algebraic method for solving systems of equations. When we know one variable's value, we can plug it into the other equation as its corresponding expression. This simplifies the system to a single linear equation that can be easily solved.",
              "source_refs": [

          "id": "SEC_25",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",
          "number": "4.3",
          "title": "Addition/Elimination",
          "order": 24,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 146,
              "page_end": 146,
              "text": "4.3\nSystems of Equations - Addition/Elimination\nObjective: Solve systems of equations using the addition/e limination\nmethod. \nWhen solving systems we have found that graphing is very limi ted when solving\nequations. We then considered a second method known as subst ituion. This is\nprobably the most used idea in solving systems in various are as of algebra. How-\never, substitution can get ugly if we don’t have a lone variab le. This leads us to\nour second method for solving systems of equations. This met hod is known as\neither Elimination or Addition. We will set up the process in the following exam-\nples, then deﬁne the ﬁve step process we can use to solve by eli mination. \nExample 177. \n3x − 4y = 8\n5 x + 4y = − 24\nNotice opposites in front of y ′s. Add columns. \n8x = − 16 Solve for x, divide by 8\n8 8\nx = − 2 We have ourx! \n5( − 2) + 4y = − 24 Plug into either original equation , simplify\n− 10 + 4y = − 24 Add 10 to both sides\n+ 10 + 10\n4y = − 14 Divide by 4\n4 4\ny = − 7\n2 Now we have oury! \n(\n− 2 , − 7\n2 \n)\nOur Solution\nIn the previous example one variable had opposites in front o f it, − 4y and 4y. \nAdding these together eliminated the y completely. This all owed us to solve for\nthe x. This is the idea behind the addition method. However, gener ally we won’t\nhave opposites in front of one of the variables. In this case w e will manipulate the\nequations to get the opposites we want by multiplying one or b oth equations (on\nboth sides!). This is shown in the next example. \nExample 178. \n− 6x + 5 y = 22\n2 x + 3y = 2 \nWe can get opposites in front ofx, by multiplying the\nsecond equation by3, to get − 6x and + 6x\n3(2 x + 3y) = (2)3 Distribute to get new second equation. \n146\n6x + 9y = 6 New second equation\n− 6x + 5 y = 22 First equation still the same, add\n14y = 28 Divide both sides by 14\n14 14\ny = 2 We have oury! \n2 x + 3(2) = 2 Plug into one of the original equations, simplify\n2 x + 6 = 2 Subtract 6 from both sides\n− 6 − 6\n2 x = − 4 Divide both sides by2 \n2 2 \nx = − 2 We also have ourx! \n( − 2 , 2) Our Solution\nWhen we looked at the x terms, − 6x and 2 x we decided to multiply the 2 x by 3\nto get the opposites we were looking for. What we are looking f or with our oppo-\nsites is the least common multiple (LCM) of the coeﬃcients. W e also could have\nsolved the above problem by looking at the terms with y, 5 y and 3y. The LCM of\n3 and 5 is 15. So we would want to multiply both equations, the 5 y by 3, and the\n3y by − 5 to get opposites, 15 y and − 15y. This illustrates an important point, \nsome problems we will have to multiply both equations by a con stant (on both\nsides) to get the opposites we want. \nExample 179. \n3x + 6y = − 9\n2 x + 9y = − 26\nWe can get opposites in front ofx, ﬁnd LCM of 6 and 9, \nThe LCM is 18. We will multiply to get 18y and − 18y\n3(3x + 6y) = ( − 9)3 Multiply the ﬁrst equation by3, both sides! \n9x + 18y = − 27\n− 2(2 x + 9y) = ( − 26)( − 2) Multiply the second equation by− 2 , both sides! \n− 4x − 18y = 52\n9x + 18y = − 27 Add two new equations together\n− 4x − 18y = 52\n5 x = 25 Divide both sides by 5 \n5 5 \nx = 5 We have our solution forx\n3(5) + 6y = − 9 Plug into either original equation, simplify\n15 + 6y = − 9 Subtract 15 from both sides\n− 15 − 15\n147"
            }
          ],

              "id": "SEC_25_C1",
              "title": "Addition/Elimination Method",
              "explanation": "The Addition/Elimination method is a technique used to solve systems of equations by adding or eliminating variables. It involves manipulating the equations to get the desired coefficients, then adding or subtracting the equations to eliminate one variable. This method is useful when graphing is not feasible and substitution is complicated due to lone variables.",

          "id": "SEC_26",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",

          "title": "Three Variables",
          "order": 25,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 151,
              "page_end": 151,
              "text": "4.4\nSystems of Equations - Three Variables\nObjective: Solve systems of equations with three variables using addi-\ntion/elimination. \nSolving systems of equations with 3 variables is very simila r to how we solve sys-\ntems with two variables. When we had two variables we reduced the system down\nto one with only one variable (by substitution or addition). With three variables\nwe will reduce the system down to one with two variables (usua lly by addition), \nwhich we can then solve by either addition or substitution. \nTo reduce from three variables down to two it is very importan t to keep the work\norganized. We will use addition with two equations to elimin ate one variable. \nThis new equation we will call (A). Then we will use a diﬀerent pair of equations\nand use addition to eliminate the same variable. This second new equation we\nwill call (B). Once we have done this we will have two equation s (A) and (B) \nwith the same two variables that we can solve using either met hod. This is shown\nin the following examples. \nExample 182. \n3x + 2 y − z = − 1 \n− 2 x − 2 y + 3z = 5 We will eliminatey using two diﬀerent pairs of equations\n5 x + 2 y − z = 3\n151\n3x + 2 y − z = − 1 Using the ﬁrst two equations, \n− 2 x − 2 y + 3z = 5 Add the ﬁrst two equations\n( A) x + 2 z = 4 This is equation ( A) , our ﬁrst equation\n− 2 x − 2 y + 3z = 5 Using the second two equations\n5 x + 2 y − z = 3 Add the second two equations\n( B) 3 x + 2 z = 8 This is equation ( B) , our second equation\n( A) x + 2 z = 4 Using ( A) and ( B) we will solve this system. \n( B) 3 x + 2 z = 8 We will solve by addition\n− 1( x + 2 z) = (4)( − 1) Multiply ( A) by − 1 \n− x − 2 z = − 4\n− x − 2 z = − 4 Add to the second equation, unchanged\n3x + 2 z = 8\n2 x = 4 Solve, divide by 2 \n2 2 \nx = 2 We now havex! Plug this into either( A) or ( B) \n(2) + 2 z = 4 We plug it into( A) , solve this equation, subtract 2 \n− 2 − 2 \n2 z = 2 Divide by 2 \n2 2 \nz = 1 We now havez! Plug this and x into any original equation\n3(2) + 2 y − (1) = − 1 We use the ﬁrst, multiply 3(2) = 6 and combine with− 1 \n2 y + 5 = − 1 Solve, subtract 5 \n− 5 − 5 \n2 y = − 6 Divide by 2 \n2 2 \ny = − 3 We now havey! \n(2 , − 3, 1) Our Solution\nAs we are solving for x, y, and z we will have an ordered triplet ( x, y, z ) instead of\n152"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_27",
          "chapter_id": "CH_5",
          "number": "4.5",
          "title": "Application: Value Problems",
          "order": 26,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 158,
              "page_end": 158,
              "text": "4.5\nSystems of Equations - Value Problems\nObjective: Solve value problems by setting up a system of equ ations. \nOne application of system of equations are known as value pro blems. Value prob-\nlems are ones where each variable has a value attached to it. F or example, if our\nvariable is the number of nickles in a person’s pocket, those nickles would have a\nvalue of ﬁve cents each. We will use a table to help us set up and solve value\nproblems. The basic structure of the table is shown below. \nNumber Value Total\nItem 1 \nItem 2 \nTotal\nThe ﬁrst column in the table is used for the number of things we have. Quite\noften, this will be our variables. The second column is used f or the that value\neach item has. The third column is used for the total value whi ch we calculate by\nmultiplying the number by the value. For example, if we have 7 dimes, each with\na value of 10 cents, the total value is 7 · 10 = 70 cents. The last row of the table is\nfor totals. We only will use the third row (also marked total) for the totals that\n158\nare given to use. This means sometimes this row may have some b lanks in it. \nOnce the table is ﬁlled in we can easily make equations by addi ng each column, \nsetting it equal to the total at the bottom of the column. This is shown in the fol-\nlowing example. \nExample 186. \nIn a child’s bank are 11 coins that have a value of S 1.85. The coins are either\nquarters or dimes. How many coins each does child have? \nNumber Value Total\nQuarter q 25\nDime d 10\nTotal\nUsing value table, use q for quarters, d for dimes\nEach quarter′s value is 25 cents, dime′s is 10 cents\nNumber Value Total\nQuarter q 25 25q\nDime d 10 10d\nTotal\nMultiply number by value to get totals\nNumber Value Total\nQuarter q 25 25q\nDime d 10 10d\nTotal 11 185\nWe have 11 coins total. This is the number total. \nWe have 1.85 for the ﬁnal total, \nWrite ﬁnal total in cents( 185) \nBecause 25 and 10 are cents\nq + d = 11 First and last columns are our equations by adding\n25q + 10d = 185 Solve by either addition or substitution . \n− 10( q + d) = ( 11 )( − 10) Using addition, multiply ﬁrst equation by− 10\n− 10q − 10d = − 110\n− 10q − 10d = − 110 Add together equations\n25q + 10d = 185\n15q = 75 Divide both sides by 15\n15 15\nq = 5 We have ourq, number of quarters is5 \n(5) + d = 11 Plug into one of original equations\n− 5 − 5 Subtract 5 from both sides\nd = 6 We have ourd, number of dimes is6\n159"
            }
          ],

            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Value Problems: A Real-World Application of Systems of Equations",
              "body": "In this lesson, we will learn how to solve value problems using systems of equations. Value problems are a type of system where each variable has a value attached to it. We will use a table to help us set up and solve these types of problems. By the end of this lesson, you should be able to explain the concept of Value Problems and its application in solving systems of equations.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Defining Value Problems",
              "body": "A value problem is a type of system of equations where each variable has a value attached to it. We use a table with three columns: the first column for the number of things we have, the second column for the value each item has, and the third column for the total value which we calculate by multiplying the number by the value.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solving a Value Problem Using Systems of Equations",
              "body": "Let's solve the following problem: In a child's bank are 11 coins that have a value of $1.85. The coins are either quarters or dimes. How many coins each does the child have? We set up the table and fill in the given information: | Number | Value | Total | | --- | --- | --- | | Quarter (q) | 25 cents | 25q | | Dime (d) | 10 cents | 10d | | Total | 11 coins | $1.85 | We have 11 coins total, so we set up the equation q + d = 11. We also know that each quarter has a value of 25 cents and each dime has a value of 10 cents, so we set up the equation 25q + 10d = 185. Now, we can solve these equations using either addition or substitution.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Error: Not Using the Correct Units",
              "body": "When solving value problems, it's easy to forget that each variable has a value attached to it and not use the correct units. For example, if we're working with dollars, we should be using $ instead of cents. If we don't use the correct units, our solution will be incorrect.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation: What are Value Problems?",
              "body": "Value problems are a type of system of equations where each variable has a value attached to it. We use a table with three columns to help us set up and solve these types of problems. The first column is for the number of things we have, the second column is for the value each item has, and the third column is for the total value which we calculate by multiplying the number by the value.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]

          "worked_examples": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_27_WE1",
              "title": "Solving a Value Problem Using Systems of Equations",
              "prompt": "In a child's bank are 11 coins that have a value of $1.85. The coins are either quarters or dimes. How many coins each does the child have?",
              "steps": [
                "When setting up the table, make sure to fill in the correct information: number of items, value of each item, and total value. | Number | Value | Total | | --- | --- | --- | | Quarter (q) | 25 cents | 25q | | Dime (d) | 10 cents | 10d | | Total | 11 coins | $1.85 Next, we set up the equation q + d = 11 and 25q + 10d = 185. We can solve these equations using either addition or substitution. Let's use addition to solve for q. Multiply the first equation by -10: -10(q + d) = (11)(-10). Next, add the two equations together: 25q + 10d = 185 and -10q - 10d = -110. This gives us 15q = 75. Now, we can divide both sides by 15 to get q = 5. Finally, plug this value back into one of the original equations to solve for d: (5) + d = 11. Subtracting 5 from both sides gives us d = 6."
              ],
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 158"
              ]
            }

          "order": 27,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 167,
              "page_end": 167,
              "text": "4.6\nSystems of Equations - Mixture Problems\nObjective: Solve mixture problems by setting up a system of e quations. \nOne application of systems of equations are mixture problem s. Mixture problems\nare ones where two diﬀerent solutions are mixed together res ulting in a new ﬁnal\nsolution. We will use the following table to help us solve mix ture problems: \nAmount Part Total\nItem 1 \nItem 2 \nFinal\nThe ﬁrst column is for the amount of each item we have. The seco nd column is\nlabeled “part”. If we mix percentages we will put the rate (wr itten as a decimal) \nin this column. If we mix prices we will put prices in this colu mn. Then we can\nmultiply the amount by the part to ﬁnd the total. Then we can ge t an equation\nby adding the amount and/or total columns that will help us so lve the problem\nand answer the questions. \nThese problems can have either one or two variables. We will s tart with one vari-\nable problems. \nExample 191. \nA chemist has 70 mL of a 50% methane solution. How much of a 80% s olution\nmust she add so the ﬁnal solution is 60% methane? \nAmount Part Total\nStart 70 0.5\nAdd x 0.8\nFinal\nSet up the mixture table. We start with 70, but\ndon′t know how much we add, that is x. The part\nis the percentages, 0.5 for start, 0.8 for add. \n167\nAmount Part Total\nStart 70 0.5\nAdd x 0.8\nFinal 70 + x 0.6\nAdd amount column to get ﬁnal amount. The\npart for this amount is 0.6 because we want the\nﬁnal solution to be 60% methane. \nAmount Part Total\nStart 70 0.5 35\nAdd x 0.8 0.8x\nFinal 70 + x 0.6 42 + 0.6x\nMultiply amount by part to get total. \nbe sure to distribute on the last row: ( 70 + x) 0.6\n35 + 0.8x = 42 + 0.6x The last column is our equation by adding\n− 0.6x − 0.6x Move variables to one side, subtract 0.6x\n35 + 0.2x = 42 Subtract 35 from both sides\n− 35 − 35\n0.2x = 7 Divide both sides by 0.2\n0.2 0.2\nx = 35 We have our x! \n35 mL must be added Our Solution\nThe same process can be used if the starting and ﬁnal amount ha ve a price\nattached to them, rather than a percentage. \nExample 192. \nA coﬀee mix is to be made that sells for S 2.50 by mixing two types of coﬀee. The\ncafe has 40 mL of coﬀee that costs S 3.00. How much of another coﬀee that costs\nS 1.50 should the cafe mix with the ﬁrst? \nAmount Part Total\nStart 40 3\nAdd x 1.5\nFinal\nSet up mixture table. We know the starting\namount and its cost, S 3. The added amount\nwe do not know but we do know its cost isS 1.50. \nAmount Part Total\nStart 40 3\nAdd x 1.5\nFinal 40 + x 2.5\nAdd the amounts to get the ﬁnal amount. \nWe want this ﬁnal amount to sell forS 2.50. \n168"
            }

              "title": "Example 191: Mixture Problem with Percentages",
              "body": "Let's solve Example 191: A chemist has 70 mL of a 50% methane solution. How much of an 80% solution must she add so the final solution is 60% methane? We set up the mixture table as follows: | Amount | Part | Total | | --- | --- | --- | | 70 | 0.5 | 35 | | x | 0.8 | 0.8x | | 70 + x | 0.6 | 42 + 0.6x | We multiply the amount by the part to get the total and then add or subtract columns to find the unknown quantity. The equation becomes: 35 + 0.2x = 42 + 0.6x We solve for x by moving variables to one side, subtracting 0.6x from both sides, and dividing both sides by 0.2. We get x = 35.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_29",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.1",
          "title": "Exponent Properties",
          "order": 28,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 177,
              "page_end": 177,
              "text": "5.1 \nPolynomials - Exponent Properties\nObjective: Simplify expressions using the properties of ex ponents. \nProblems with expoenents can often be simpliﬁed using a few b asic exponent\nproperties. Exponents represent repeated multiplication . We will use this fact to\ndiscover the important properties. \nWorld View Note: The word exponent comes from the Latin “expo” meaning\nout of and “ponere” meaning place. While there is some debate , it seems that the\nBabylonians living in Iraq were the ﬁrst to do work with expon ents (dating back\nto the 23rd century BC or earlier!) \nExample 196. \na3a2 Expand exponents to multiplication problem\n( aaa )( aa) Now we have5 a′s being multiplied together\na5 Our Solution\nA quicker method to arrive at our answer would have been to jus t add the expo-\nnents: a3a2 = a3+2 = a5 This is known as the product rule of exponents\nProduct Rule of Exponents: aman = am+ n\nThe product rule of exponents can be used to simplify many pro blems. We will\nadd the exponent on like variables. This is shown in the follo wing examples\nExample 197. \n32 · 36 · 3 Same base, add the exponents 2 + 6 + 1 \n39 Our Solution\nExample 198. \n2 x3y5z · 5 xy 2 z3 Multiply 2 · 5 , add exponents on x, y and z\n10x4y7z4 Our Solution\nRather than multiplying, we will now try to divide with expon ents\nExample 199. \na5\na2 \nExpand exponents\naaaaa\naa Divide out two of thea′s \naaa Convert to exponents\na3 Our Solution\n177\nA quicker method to arrive at the solution would have been to j ust subtract the\nexponents, a5 \na2 = a5− 2 = a3. This is known as the quotient rule of exponents. \nQuotient Rule of Exponents: am\nan = am− n\nThe quotient rule of exponents can similarly be used to simpl ify exponent prob-\nlems by subtracting exponents on like variables. This is sho wn in the following\nexamples. \nExample 200. \n713\n75\nSame base, subtract the exponents\n78 Our Solution\nExample 201. \n5 a3b5c2 \n2 ab3c Subtract exponents on a, b and c\n5 \n2 \na2 b2 c Our Solution\nA third property we will look at will have an exponent problem raised to a second\nexponent. This is investigated in the following example. \nExample 202. \n( a2 ) 3\nThis means we havea2 three times\na2 · a2 · a2 Add exponents\na6 Our solution\nA quicker method to arrive at the solution would have been to j ust multiply the\nexponents, ( a2 ) 3 = a2 · 3 = a6. This is known as the power of a power rule of expo-\nnents. \nPower of a Power Rule of Exponents: ( am) n = amn\nThis property is often combined with two other properties wh ich we will investi-\ngate now. \nExample 203. \n( ab) 3 This means we have( ab) three times\n( ab)( ab)( ab) Three a′s and three b′s can be written with exponents\na3b3 Our Solution\n178"
            }

          "id": "SEC_30",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.2",
          "title": "Negative Exponents",
          "order": 29,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 183,
              "page_end": 183,
              "text": "5.2\nPolynomials - Negative Exponents\nObjective: Simplify expressions with negative exponents u sing the\nproperties of exponents. \nThere are a few special exponent properties that deal with ex ponents that are not\npositive. The ﬁrst is considered in the following example, w hich is worded out 2\ndiﬀerent ways: \nExample 214. \na3\na3\nUse the quotient rule to subtract exponents\na0 Our Solution, but now we consider the problema the second way: \na3\na3 Rewrite exponents as repeated multiplication\naaa\naaa\nReduce out all thea′s \n1 \n1 \n= 1 Our Solution, when we combine the two solutions we get: \na0 = 1 Our ﬁnal result. \nThis ﬁnal result is an imporant property known as the zero pow er rule of expo-\nnents\nZero Power Rule of Exponents: a0 = 1 \nAny number or expression raised to the zero power will always be 1. This is illus-\ntrated in the following example. \nExample 215. \n(3x2 ) 0 Zero power rule\n1 Our Solution\nAnother property we will consider here deals with negative e xponents. Again we\nwill solve the following example two ways. \n183\nExample 216. \na3\na5 Using the quotient rule, subtract exponents\na− 2 Our Solution, but we will also solve this problem another way. \na3\na5 Rewrite exponents as repeated multiplication\naaa\naaaaa Reduce three a′s out of top and bottom\n1 \naa Simplify to exponents\n1 \na2 Our Solution, putting these solutions together gives: \na− 2 = 1 \na2 Our Final Solution\nThis example illustrates an important property of exponent s. Negative exponents\nyield the reciprocal of the base. Once we take the reciprical the exponent is now\npositive. Also, it is important to note a negative exponent d oes not mean the\nexpression is negative, only that we need the reciprocal of t he base. Following are\nthe rules of negative exponents\nRules of Negative Exponets: \na− m = 1 \nm\n1 \na− m = am\n( a\nb\n) − m\n= bm\nam\nNegative exponents can be combined in several diﬀerent ways . As a general rule if\nwe think of our expression as a fraction, negative exponents in the numerator\nmust be moved to the denominator, likewise, negative expone nts in the denomi-\nnator need to be moved to the numerator. When the base with exp onent moves, \nthe exponent is now positive. This is illustrated in the foll owing example. \nExample 217. \na3b− 2 c\n2 d− 1 e− 4f 2 Negative exponents on b, d, and e need to ﬂip\na 3cde 4\n2 b2 f 2 Our Solution\n184"
            }
          ],

              "explanation": "In mathematics, a negative exponent represents the reciprocal of the base. For example, if we have an expression like 1/a, we can rewrite it as a^(-1). This means that when we take the reciprocal of a number or expression, the exponent becomes positive.",

              "title": "Rules for Negative Exponents",
              "explanation": "There are three main rules for negative exponents: a^(-m) = 1/m * a^m, (a/b)^(-m) = b^m / a^m, and (ab)^(-m) = b^m/a^m. These rules allow us to simplify expressions with negative exponents by rewriting them in terms of positive exponents.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_31",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.3",
          "title": "Scientiﬁc Notation",
          "order": 30,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 188,
              "page_end": 188,
              "text": "5.3\nPolynomials - Scientiﬁc Notation\nObjective: Multiply and divide expressions using scientiﬁ c notation and\nexponent properties. \nOne application of exponent properties comes from scientiﬁ c notation. Scientiﬁc\nnotation is used to represent really large or really small nu mbers. An example of\nreally large numbers would be the distance that light travel s in a year in miles. \nAn example of really small numbers would be the mass of a singl e hydrogen atom\nin grams. Doing basic operations such as multiplication and division with these\nnumbers would normally be very combersome. However, our exp onent properties\nmake this process much simpler. \nFirst we will take a look at what scientiﬁc notation is. Scien tiﬁc notation has two\nparts, a number between one and ten (it can be equal to one, but not ten), and\nthat number multiplied by ten to some exponent. \nScientiﬁc Notation: a × 10b where 1 ⩽ a < 10\nThe exponent, b, is very important to how we convert between scientiﬁc notat ion\nand normal numbers, or standard notation. The exponent tell s us how many\ntimes we will multiply by 10. Multiplying by 10 in aﬀect moves the decimal point\none place. So the exponent will tell us how many times the expo nent moves\nbetween scientiﬁc notation and standard notation. To decid e which direction to\nmove the decimal (left or right) we simply need to remember th at positive expo-\nnents mean in standard notation we have a big number (bigger t han ten) and neg-\native exponents mean in standard notation we have a small num ber (less than\none). \nKeeping this in mind, we can easily make conversions between standard notation\nand scientiﬁc notation. \nExample 221. \nConvert 14, 200 to scientiﬁc notation Put decimal after ﬁrst nonzero num ber\n1.42 Exponent is how many times decimal moved , 4\n× 104 Positive exponent, standard notation is big\n1.42 × 104 Our Solution\nExample 222. \nConvert 0.0042 to scientiﬁc notation Put decimal after ﬁrst nonzero number\n4.2 Exponent is how many times decimal moved , 3\n× 10− 3 Negative exponent, standard notation is small\n4.2 × 10− 3 Our Solution\n188\nExample 223. \nConvert 3.21 × 105 to standard notation Positive exponent means standard nota tion\nbig number. Move decimal right5 places\n321 , 000 Our Solution\nExample 224. \nConver 7.4× 10− 3 to standard notation Negative exponent means standard nota tion\nis a small number. Move decimal left3 places\n0.0074 Our Solution\nConverting between standard notation and scientiﬁc notati on is important to\nunderstand how scientiﬁc notation works and what it does. He re our main\ninterest is to be able to multiply and divide numbers in scien tiﬁc notation using\nexponent properties. The way we do this is ﬁrst do the operati on with the front\nnumber (multiply or divide) then use exponent properties to simplify the 10’s. \nScientiﬁc notation is the only time where it will be allowed t o have negative expo-\nnents in our ﬁnal solution. The negative exponent simply inf orms us that we are\ndealing with small numbers. Consider the following example s. \nExample 225. \n( 2.1 × 10− 7)( 3.7 × 105) Deal with numbers and 10′s separately\n( 2.1 )( 3.7) = 7.77 Multiply numbers\n10− 7105 = 10− 2 Use product rule on 10′s and add exponents\n7.77 × 10− 2 Our Solution\nExample 226. \n4.96 × 104\n3.1 × 10− 3\nDeal with numbers and 10′s separately\n4.96\n3.1 = 1.6 Divide Numbers\n104\n10− 3 = 107 Use quotient rule to subtract exponents, be careful with negatives! \nBe careful with negatives, 4 − ( − 3) = 4 + 3 = 7\n1.6 × 107 Our Solution\n189"
            }
          ],

          ],

              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes with Exponents",
              "body": "When using exponent properties, be careful not to mix up the product and quotient rules. Remember that when multiplying numbers in scientific notation, we add the exponents of 10 (e.g., 3 × 10^2 × 4 × 10^5 = 12 × 10^7). However, when dividing numbers in scientific notation, we subtract the exponents of 10 (e.g., (3 × 10^2) / (4 × 10^5) = 0.75 × 10^(−3)).",
              "source_refs": [

            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_31_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Reflecting on Scientific Notation",
              "body": "Take a moment to review the concept of scientific notation and its importance in representing large or small numbers. Think about how you can apply this knowledge to real-world problems and make connections between different mathematical concepts.",

              ]

        {
          "id": "SEC_32",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.4",
          "title": "Introduction to Polynomials",
          "order": 31,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 192,
              "page_end": 192,
              "text": "5.4\nPolynomials - Introduction to Polynomials\nObjective: Evaluate, add, and subtract polynomials. \nMany applications in mathematics have to do with what are cal led polynomials. \nPolynomials are made up of terms. Terms are a product of numbers and/or vari-\nables. For example, 5 x, 2 y2 , − 5 , ab 3c, and x are all terms. Terms are connected\nto each other by addition or subtraction. Expressions are of ten named based on\nthe number of terms in them. A monomial has one term, such as 3x2 . A bino-\nmial has two terms, such as a2 − b2 . A Trinomial has three terms, such as ax 2 + \nbx + c. The term polynomial means many terms. Monomials, binomials, trino-\nmials, and expressions with more terms all fall under the umb rella of “polyno-\nmials”. \nIf we know what the variable in a polynomial represents we can replace the vari-\nable with the number and evaluate the polynomial as shown in t he following\nexample. \nExample 230. \n2 x2 − 4x + 6 when x = − 4 Replace variablex with − 4\n2( − 4) 2 − 4( − 4) + 6 Exponents ﬁrst\n2( 16) − 4( − 4) + 6 Multiplication ( we can do all terms at once) \n32 + 16 + 6 Add\n54 Our Solution\nIt is important to be careful with negative variables and exp onents. Remember\nthe exponent only eﬀects the number it is physically attache d to. This means − \n32 = − 9 because the exponent is only attached to the 3. Also, ( − 3) 2 = 9 because\nthe exponent is attached to the parenthesis and eﬀects every thing inside. When\nwe replace a variable with parenthesis like in the previous e xample, the substi-\ntuted value is in parenthesis. So the ( − 4) 2 = 16 in the example. However, con-\nsider the next example. \nExample 231. \n− x2 + 2 x + 6 when x = 3 Replace variablex with 3\n− (3) 2 + 2(3) + 6 Exponent only on the3, not negative\n− 9 + 2(3) + 6 Multiply\n− 9 + 6 + 6 Add\n3 Our Solution\n192\nWorld View Note: Ada Lovelace in 1842 described a Diﬀerence Engine that\nwould be used to caluclate values of polynomials. Her work be came the founda-\ntion for what would become the modern computer (the programm ing language\nAda was named in her honor), more than 100 years after her deat h from cancer. \nGenerally when working with polynomials we do not know the va lue of the vari-\nable, so we will try and simplify instead. The simplest opera tion with polynomials\nis addition. When adding polynomials we are mearly combinin g like terms. Con-\nsider the following example\nExample 232. \n(4x3 − 2 x + 8) + (3x3 − 9x2 − 11 ) Combine like terms4x3 + 3x3 and 8 − 11 \n7x3 − 9x2 − 2 x − 3 Our Solution\nGenerally ﬁnal answers for polynomials are written so the ex ponent on the vari-\nable counts down. Example 3 demonstrates this with the expon ent counting down\n3, 2, 1, 0 (recall x0 = 1 ). Subtracting polynomials is almost as fast. One extra step\ncomes from the minus in front of the parenthesis. When we have a negative in\nfront of parenthesis we distribute it through, changing the signs of everything\ninside. The same is done for the subtraction sign. \nExample 233. \n(5 x2 − 2 x + 7) − (3x2 + 6x − 4) Distribute negative through second part\n5 x2 − 2 x + 7 − 3x2 − 6x + 4 Combine like terms5 x2 − 3x3, − 2 x − 6x, and 7 + 4\n2 x2 − 8x + 11 Our Solution\nAddition and subtraction can also be combined into the same p roblem as shown\nin this ﬁnal example. \nExample 234. \n(2 x2 − 4x + 3) + (5 x2 − 6x + 1) − ( x2 − 9x + 8) Distribute negative through\n2 x2 − 4x + 3 + 5 x2 − 6x + 1 − x2 + 9x − 8 Combine like terms\n6x2 − x − 4 Our Solution\n193"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_32"

              "body": "Let's evaluate the polynomial 2x^2 - 4x + 6 when x = -4. First, replace x with -4: 2(-4)^2 - 4(-4) + 6. Next, simplify the expressions inside parentheses: 2(16) - 4(-4) + 6. Then, multiply and divide from left to right: 32 - 16 + 6 = 22.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_33",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.5",
          "title": "Multiply Polynomials",
          "order": 32,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 196,
              "page_end": 196,
              "text": "5.5\nPolynomials - Multiplying Polynomials\nObjective: Multiply polynomials. \nMultiplying polynomials can take several diﬀerent forms ba sed on what we are\nmultiplying. We will ﬁrst look at multiplying monomials, th en monomials by\npolynomials and ﬁnish with polynomials by polynomials. \nMultiplying monomials is done by multiplying the numbers or coeﬃcients and\nthen adding the exponents on like factors. This is shown in th e next example. \nExample 235. \n(4x3y4z)(2 x2 y6z3) Multiply numbers and add exponents forx, y, and z\n8x5y10z4 Our Solution\nIn the previous example it is important to remember that the z has an exponent\nof 1 when no exponent is written. Thus for our answer the z has an exponent of\n1 + 3 = 4. Be very careful with exponents in polynomials. If we are add ing or sub-\ntracting the exponnets will stay the same, but when we multip ly (or divide) the\nexponents will be changing. \nNext we consider multiplying a monomial by a polynomial. We h ave seen this\noperation before with distributing through parenthesis. H ere we will see the exact\nsame process. \nExample 236. \n4x3(5 x2 − 2 x + 5) Distribute the 4x3, multiplying numbers, adding exponents\n20x5 − 8x4 + 20x3 Our Solution\nFollowing is another example with more variables. When dist ributing the expo-\nnents on a are added and the exponents on b are added. \nExample 237. \n2 a3b(3ab2 − 4a) Distribute, multiplying numbers and adding exponents\n6a4b3 − 8a4b Our Solution\nThere are several diﬀerent methods for multiplying polynom ials. All of which\nwork, often students prefer the method they are ﬁrst taught. Here three methods\nwill be discussed. All three methods will be used to solve the same two multipli-\ncation problems. \nMultiply by Distributing\n196\nJust as we distribute a monomial through parenthesis we can d istribute an entire\npolynomial. As we do this we take each term of the second polyn omial and put it\nin front of the ﬁrst polynomial. \nExample 238. \n(4x + 7y)(3x − 2 y) Distribute (4x + 7y) through parenthesis\n3x(4x + 7y) − 2 y(4x + 7y) Distribute the 3x and − 2 y\n12x2 + 21 xy − 8xy − 14y2 Combine like terms 21 xy − 8xy\n12x2 + 13xy − 14y2 Our Solution\nThis example illustrates an important point, the negative/ subtraction sign stays\nwith the 2 y. Which means on the second step the negative is also distribu ted\nthrough the last set of parenthesis. \nMultiplying by distributing can easily be extended to probl ems with more terms. \nFirst distribute the front parenthesis onto each term, then distribute again! \nExample 239. \n(2 x − 5)(4x2 − 7x + 3) Distribute (2 x − 5) through parenthesis\n4x2 (2 x − 5) − 7x(2 x − 5) + 3(2 x − 5) Distribute again through each parenthesis\n8x3 − 20x2 − 14x2 + 35x + 6x − 15 Combine like terms\n8x3 − 34x2 + 41 x − 15 Our Solution\nThis process of multiplying by distributing can easily be re versed to do an impor-\ntant procedure known as factoring. Factoring will be addres sed in a future lesson. \nMultiply by FOIL\nAnother form of multiplying is known as FOIL. Using the FOIL m ethod we mul-\ntiply each term in the ﬁrst binomial by each term in the second binomial. The\nletters of FOIL help us remember every combination. F stands for First, we mul-\ntiply the ﬁrst term of each binomial. O stand for Outside, we m ultiply the outside\ntwo terms. I stands for Inside, we multiply the inside two ter ms. L stands for\nLast, we multiply the last term of each binomial. This is show n in the next\nexample: \nExample 240. \n(4x + 7y)(3x − 2 y) Use FOIL to multiply\n(4x)(3x) = 12x2 F − First terms (4x)(3x) \n(4x)( − 2 y) = − 8xy O − Outside terms (4x)( − 2 y) \n(7y)(3x) = 21 xy I − Inside terms (7y)(3x) \n(7y)( − 2 y) = − 14y2 L − Last terms (7y)( − 2 y) \n12x2 − 8xy + 21 xy − 14y2 Combine like terms − 8xy + 21 xy\n12x2 + 13xy − 14y2 Our Solution\n197"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_33"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Multiply Polynomials well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",

            "Apply the correct method for multiplying polynomials, including FOIL"

              "id": "SEC_33_C1",
              "title": "The Distributive Property of Polynomials",
              "explanation": "When we multiply a polynomial by another polynomial, we can use the distributive property to break down the product into simpler terms. This involves distributing each term in one polynomial across each term in the other polynomial. For example, when multiplying (4x + 7y) and (3x - 2y), we would first distribute the terms of the second polynomial across each term in the first polynomial.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 196"

              "id": "SEC_33_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why Multiply Polynomials?",
              "body": "Polynomials are used to represent algebraic expressions that have multiple variables and coefficients. When we multiply polynomials, we are essentially combining these expressions in different ways to create new products. This is a fundamental operation in algebra and has numerous applications in various fields such as physics, engineering, and computer science.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 196"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_33_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Multiplying Polynomials",
              "body": "To multiply two polynomials, we use the distributive property or the FOIL method, depending on the form of the polynomials. When multiplying monomials, we simply multiply the coefficients together and add the exponents. The result is a new polynomial that represents the product of the original expressions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 196"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_33_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example 1: Multiplying Monomials",
              "body": "Let's say we want to multiply (4x^3 y^4 z) by (2 x^2 y^6 z^3). Using the rule for multiplying monomials, we get: (4x^3 y^4 z) * (2 x^2 y^6 z^3) = 8 x^(3+2) y^(4+6) z^(1+3) = 8 x^5 y^10 z^4 So the product of these two monomials is 8x^5y^10z^4.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 196"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_33_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Incorrectly Adding Exponents",
              "body": "When multiplying polynomials, it's easy to get confused about how to handle exponents. A common mistake is adding the exponents of like variables instead of multiplying them. For example, in the expression (4x^3 y^4 z) * (2 x^2 y^6 z^3), if we incorrectly add the exponents, we would get 8 x^(3+2) y^(4+6) = 8 x^5 y^{10} instead of 8 x^5 y^{10} z^4. Remember to multiply the exponents when multiplying like variables.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 196"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_33_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation: FOIL Method",
              "body": "The FOIL method is a shortcut for multiplying two binomials. To use it, we simply multiply the first terms, then the outside terms, then the inside terms, and finally the last terms. The letters of FOIL stand for First, Outside, Inside, and Last. Remember to follow this order when using the FOIL method.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 196"
              ]
            }

              "id": "SEC_33_WE1",

              "prompt": "Multiply (4x + 7y) by (3x - 2y)",
              "steps": [

          "number": "5.6",
          "title": "Multiply Special Products",
          "order": 33,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 201,
              "page_end": 201,
              "text": "5.6\nPolynomials - Multiply Special Products\nObjective: Recognize and use special product rules of a sum a nd diﬀer-\nence and perfect squares to multiply polynomials. \nThere are a few shortcuts that we can take when multiplying po lynomials. If we\ncan recognize them the shortcuts can help us arrive at the sol ution much quicker. \nThese shortcuts will also be useful to us as our study of algeb ra continues. \nThe ﬁrst shortcut is often called a sum and a diﬀerence . A sum and a diﬀer-\nence is easily recognized as the numbers and variables are ex actly the same, but\nthe sign in the middle is diﬀerent (one sum, one diﬀerence). T o illustrate the\nshortcut consider the following example, multiplied by the distributing method. \nExample 246. \n( a + b)( a − b) Distribute ( a + b) \na( a + b) − b( a + b) Distribute a and − b\na2 + ab − ab − b2 Combine like termsab − ab\na2 − b2 Our Solution\nThe important part of this example is the middle terms subtra cted to zero. \nRather than going through all this work, when we have a sum and a diﬀerence we\nwill jump right to our solution by squaring the ﬁrst term and s quaring the last\nterm, putting a subtraction between them. This is illustrat ed in the following\nexample\nExample 247. \n( x − 5)( x + 5) Recognize sum and diﬀerence\nx2 − 25 Square both , put subtraction between. Our Solution\nThis is much quicker than going through the work of multiplyi ng and combining\nlike terms. Often students ask if they can just multiply out u sing another method\nand not learn the shortcut. These shortcuts are going to be ve ry useful when we\nget to factoring polynomials, or reversing the multiplicat ion process. For this\nreason it is very important to be able to recognize these shor tcuts. More examples\nare shown here. \n201\nExample 248. \n(3x + 7)(3x − 7) Recognize sum and diﬀerence\n9x2 − 49 Square both , put subtraction between. Our Solution\nExample 249. \n(2 x − 6y)(2 x + 6y) Recognize sum and diﬀerence\n4x2 − 36y2 Square both, put subtraction between. Our Solution\nIt is interesting to note that while we can multiply and get an answer like a2 − b2 \n(with subtraction), it is impossible to multiply real numbe rs and end up with a\nproduct such as a2 + b2 (with addition). \nAnother shortcut used to multiply is known as a perfect square . These are easy\nto recognize as we will have a binomial with a 2 in the exponent. The following\nexample illustrates multiplying a perfect square\nExample 250. \n( a + b) 2 Squared is same as multiplying by itself\n( a + b)( a + b) Distribute ( a + b) \na( a + b) + b( a + b) Distribute again through ﬁnal parenthesis\na2 + ab + ab + b2 Combine like termsab + ab\na2 + 2 ab + b2 Our Solution\nThis problem also helps us ﬁnd our shortcut for multiplying. The ﬁrst term in the\nanswer is the square of the ﬁrst term in the problem. The middl e term is 2 times\nthe ﬁrst term times the second term. The last term is the squar e of the last term. \nThis can be shortened to square the ﬁrst, twice the product, s quare the last. If we\ncan remember this shortcut we can square any binomial. This i s illustrated in the\nfollowing example\nExample 251. \n( x − 5) 2 Recognize perfect square\nx2 Square the ﬁrst\n2( x)( − 5) = − 10x Twice the product\n( − 5) 2 = 25 Square the last\nx2 − 10x + 25 Our Solution\n202"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_34"
          ],

              "id": "SEC_34_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why Multiply Special Products Matters",
              "body": "Multiply special products is a fundamental concept in algebra that will be used extensively throughout our study. Recognizing sum and difference, and perfect squares allows us to quickly multiply polynomials without going through the tedious work of distributing and combining like terms. These shortcuts will also be useful when we get to factoring polynomials and reversing the multiplication process.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 201"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_34_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Multiply Special Products",
              "body": "To multiply special products, we use two shortcuts: sum and difference, and perfect squares. For a sum and difference, we square the first term and subtract it from the square of the last term. For a perfect square, we square the first term, twice the product, and square the last term.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 201"
              ]
            },

              "id": "SEC_34_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Multiplying Special Products: Sum and Difference",
              "body": "Let's multiply (x - 5)(x + 5) using the sum and difference shortcut. We square the first term x2, put a subtraction between, and then square the last term to get x2 - 25.",
              "source_refs": [

              ]
            },

              "title": "Common Mistake: Not Squaring Both Terms",
              "body": "A common mistake students make is not squaring both terms when using the sum and difference shortcut.         {
          "id": "SEC_35",
          "chapter_id": "CH_6",
          "number": "5.7",
          "title": "Divide Polynomials",
          "order": 34,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 205,
              "page_end": 205,
              "text": "5.7\nPolynomials - Divide Polynomials\nObjective: Divide polynomials using long division. \nDividing polynomials is a process very similar to long divis ion of whole numbers. \nBut before we look at that, we will ﬁrst want to be able to maste r dividing a\npolynomial by a monomial. The way we do this is very similar to distributing, \nbut the operation we distribute is the division, dividing ea ch term by the mono-\nmial and reducing the resulting expression. This is shown in the following exam-\nples\nExample 256. \n9x5 + 6x4 − 18x3 − 24x2 \n3x2 \nDivide each term in the numerator by3x2 \n9x5\n3x2 + 6x4\n3x2 − 18x3\n3x2 − 24x2 \n3x2 Reduce each fraction, subtracting exponents\n3x3 + 2 x2 − 6x − 8 Our Solution\nExample 257. \n8x3 + 4x2 − 2 x + 6\n4x2 Divide each term in the numerator by4x2 \n8x3\n4x2 + 4x2 \n4x2 − 2 x\n4x2 + 6\n4x2 Reduce each fraction, subtracting exponents\nRemember negative exponents are moved to denominator\n2 x + 1 − 1 \n2 x + 3\n2 x2 Our Solution\nThe previous example illustrates that sometimes we will hav e fractions in our\nsolution, as long as they are reduced this will be correct for our solution. Also\ninteresting in this problem is the second term 4x2 \n4x2 divided out completely. \nRemember that this means the reduced answer is 1 not 0. \nLong division is required when we divide by more than just a mo nomial. Long\ndivision with polynomials works very similar to long divisi on with whole numbers. \n205\nAn example is given to review the (general) steps that are use d with whole num-\nbers that we will also use with polynomials\nExample 258. \n4| 631 \nDivide front numbers: 6\n4\n= 1 / \n1 \n4| 631 Multiply this number by divisor: 1 · 4 = 4\n− 4 Change the sign of this number ( make it subtract) and combine\n23 Bring down next number\n1 5 Repeat, divide front numbers: 23\n4 = 5 / \n4| 631 \n− 4\n23 Multiply this number by divisor : 5 · 4 = 20\n− 20 Change the sign of this number( make it subtract) and combine\n31 Bring down next number\n157 Repeat, divide front numbers: 31 \n4 = 7/ \n4| 631 \n− 4\n23\n− 20\n31 Multiply this number by divisor : 7 · 4 = 28\n− 28 Change the sign of this number( make it subtract) and combine\n3 We will write our remainder asa fraction, over the divisor, added to the end\n157 3\n4\nOur Solution\nThis same process will be used to multiply polynomials. The o nly diﬀerence is we\nwill replace the word “number” with the word “term”\nDividing Polynomials\n1. Divide front terms\n2. Multiply this term by the divisor\n206"
            }
          ],

          "lesson_blocks": [

              "id": "SEC_35_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why Divide Polynomials?",
              "body": "Dividing polynomials is a fundamental concept in algebra that allows us to simplify complex expressions and solve equations. It's essential to understand polynomial division to apply it to more advanced topics, such as solving systems of equations and working with rational functions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 205"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_35_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "The Process of Polynomial Division",
              "body": "Polynomial division involves the following steps: 1) Divide front terms, 2) Multiply this term by the divisor, and 3) Reduce each fraction. This process is similar to long division with whole numbers. The key difference is that we replace the word 'number' with the word 'term'.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 205"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_35_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example: Divide 9x^5 + 6x^4 - 18x^3 - 24x^2 by 3x^2",
              "body": "To divide this polynomial, we follow the steps outlined above. First, we divide each term in the numerator by the divisor: 9x^5 / 3x^2 = 3x^3, 6x^4 / 3x^2 = 2x^2, -18x^3 / 3x^2 = -6x, and -24x^2 / 3x^2 = -8. Then, we reduce each fraction: 3x^3 + 2x^2 - 6x - 8.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 205"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_35_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Failing to Reduce Fractions",
              "body": "A common mistake students make when dividing polynomials is failing to reduce fractions. For example, in the previous example, we obtained 3x^3 + 2x^2 - 6x - 8 as our solution. However, we forgot to reduce the fraction 2x^2 / 3x^2 = x^0 = 1. This can lead to incorrect solutions and answers.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 205"
              ]
            },
            {
