              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Reflection: Understanding Polynomial Division",
              "body": "To ensure you understand polynomial division, make sure to follow the steps outlined above: divide front terms, multiply this term by the divisor, and reduce each fraction. Practice dividing polynomials with different divisors and numerators to reinforce your understanding.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 205"
              ]
            }

    {
      "id": "CH_7",
      "number": "6",
      "title": "Factoring",
      "order": 6,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_36",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.1",
          "title": "Greatest Common Factor",
          "order": 35,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 212,
              "page_end": 212,
              "text": "6.1 \nFactoring - Greatest Common Factor\nObjective: Find the greatest common factor of a polynomial a nd factor\nit out of the expression. \nThe opposite of multiplying polynomials together is factor ing polynomials. There\nare many beniﬁts of a polynomial being factored. We use facto red polynomials to\nhelp us solve equations, learn behaviors of graphs, work wit h fractions and more. \nBecause so many concepts in algebra depend on us being able to factor polyno-\nmials it is very important to have very strong factoring skil ls. \nIn this lesson we will focus on factoring using the greatest c ommon factor or GCF\nof a polynomial. When we multiplied polynomials, we multipl ied monomials by\npolynomials by distributing, solving problems such as 4x2 (2 x2 − 3x + 8) = 8 x4 − \n12x3 + 32x. In this lesson we will work the same problem backwards. We wi ll\nstart with 8x2 − 12x3 + 32x and try and work backwards to the 4x2 (2 x − 3x + 8) . \nTo do this we have to be able to ﬁrst identify what is the GCF of a polynomial. \nWe will ﬁrst introduce this by looking at ﬁnding the GCF of sev eral numbers. To\nﬁnd a GCF of sevearal numbers we are looking for the largest nu mber that can be\ndivided by each of the numbers. This can often be done with qui ck mental math\nand it is shown in the following example\nExample 262. \nFind the GCF of 15, 24, and 27\n15\n3\n= 5 , 24\n3 = 6, 27\n3 = 9 Each of the numbers can be divided by3\nGCF = 3 Our Solution\nWhen there are variables in our problem we can ﬁrst ﬁnd the GCF of the num-\n212\nbers using mental math, then we take any variables that are in common with each\nterm, using the lowest exponent. This is shown in the next exa mple\nExample 263. \nGCF of 24x4y2 z , 18x2 y4, and 12x3yz 5\n24\n6 = 4, 18\n6 = 3, 12\n6 = 2 Each number can be divided by6\nx2 y x and y are in all3, using lowest exponets\nGCF = 6x2 y Our Solution\nTo factor out a GCF from a polynomial we ﬁrst need to identify t he GCF of all\nthe terms, this is the part that goes in front of the parenthes is, then we divide\neach term by the GCF, the answer is what is left inside the pare nthesis. This is\nshown in the following examples\nExample 264. \n4x2 − 20x + 16 GCF is 4, divide each term by4\n4x2 \n4\n= x2 , − 20x\n4 = − 5 x, 16\n4 = 4 This is what is left inside the parenthesis\n4( x2 − 5 x + 4) Our Solution\nWith factoring we can always check our solutions by multiply ing (distributing in\nthis case) out the answer and the solution should be the origi nal equation. \nExample 265. \n25x4 − 15x3 + 20x2 GCF is 5 x2 , divide each term by this\n25x4\n5 x2 = 5 x2 , − 15x3\n5 x2 = − 3x, 20x2 \n5 x2 = 4 This is what is left inside the parenthesis\n5 x2 (5 x2 − 3x + 4) Our Solution\nExample 266. \n3x3y2 z + 5 x4y3z5 − 4xy 4 GCF is xy 2 , divide each term by this\n213"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_36_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "The Core Idea of Greatest Common Factor",
              "body": "Imagine you have a set of numbers, such as 12, 18, and 24. You want to find the largest number that can divide each of these numbers without leaving a remainder. This is what we call the greatest common factor (GCF). In algebra, finding the GCF is crucial for factoring polynomials, which helps us solve equations, graph functions, and more.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 212"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_36_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Greatest Common Factor",
              "body": "To find the GCF of a set of numbers, we look for the largest number that can divide each of the numbers without leaving a remainder. We can use mental math or division to find the GCF. For polynomials with variables, we take any common variables and use their lowest exponents.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 212"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_36_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Finding the Greatest Common Factor",
              "body": "Example: Find the GCF of 24x^4y^2z, 18x^2y^4, and 12x^3yz^5. We first find the GCF of the numbers using mental math or division. Then, we take any common variables (in this case, x, y, and z) and use their lowest exponents. The GCF is therefore 6x^2y.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 212"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_36_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes with Greatest Common Factor",
              "body": "One common mistake when finding the GCF is to choose a smaller number that can divide all the numbers, rather than the largest possible number. For example, in the previous problem, some students might choose 2x^2y as the GCF instead of 6x^2y. This is incorrect because 2x^2y is not the greatest common factor.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 212"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_36_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation: Greatest Common Factor",
              "body": "Take a moment to reflect on what you've learned about finding the GCF of numbers and polynomials. Think about how you can apply this concept to simplify expressions, solve equations, or analyze graph behaviors. Can you think of examples where the GCF is useful in real-world applications?",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 212"
              ]
            }
          ],
          "worked_examples": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_36_WE1",
              "title": "Factoring a Polynomial using Greatest Common Factor",
              "prompt": "Factor out the greatest common factor from the polynomial: 4x^2 - 20x + 16",
              "steps": [
                "Step 1: Identify the GCF of the terms. In this case, it's 4.",
                "Step 2: Divide each term by the GCF (4). This gives us x^2, -5x, and 4, respectively.",
                "Step 3: Write the factored form by placing the GCF in front of the parentheses: 4(x^2 - 5x + 4)"
              ],
              "source_refs": [

              ]
            }

        {
          "id": "SEC_37",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.2",
          "title": "Grouping",
          "order": 36,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 216,
              "page_end": 216,
              "text": "6.2\nFactoring - Grouping\nObjective: Factor polynomials with four terms using groupi ng. \nThe ﬁrst thing we will always do when factoring is try to facto r out a GCF. This\nGCF is often a monomial like in the problem 5 x y + 10x z the GCF is the mono-\nmial 5 x, so we would have 5 x( y + 2 z) . However, a GCF does not have to be a\nmonomial, it could be a binomial. To see this, consider the fo llowing two example. \nExample 270. \n3ax − 7bx Both havex in common, factor it out\nx(3a − 7b) Our Solution\nNow the same problem, but instead of x we have (2 a + 5 b) . \nExample 271. \n3a(2 a + 5 b) − 7b(2 a + 5 b) Both have(2 a + 5 b) in common, factor it out\n(2 a + 5 b)(3a − 7b) Our Solution\nIn the same way we factored out a GCF of x we can factor out a GCF which is a\nbinomial, (2 a + 5 b) . This process can be extended to factor problems where there\nis no GCF to factor out, or after the GCF is factored out, there is more factoring\nthat can be done. Here we will have to use another strategy to f actor. We will use\na process known as grouping. Grouping is how we will factor if there are four\nterms in the problem. Remember, factoring is like multiplyi ng in reverse, so ﬁrst\nwe will look at a multiplication problem and then try to rever se the process. \nExample 272. \n(2 a + 3)(5 b + 2) Distribute (2 a + 3) into second parenthesis\n5 b(2 a + 3) + 2(2 a + 3) Distribute each monomial\n10ab + 15b + 4a + 6 Our Solution\nThe solution has four terms in it. We arrived at the solution b y looking at the\ntwo parts, 5 b(2 a + 3) and 2(2 a + 3) . When we are factoring by grouping we will\nalways divide the problem into two parts, the ﬁrst two terms a nd the last two\nterms. Then we can factor the GCF out of both the left and right sides. When we\ndo this our hope is what is left in the parenthesis will match o n both the left and\nright. If they match we can pull this matching GCF out front, p utting the rest in\nparenthesis and we will be factored. The next example is the s ame problem\nworked backwards, factoring instead of multiplying. \n216\nExample 273. \n10ab + 15b + 4a + 6 Split problem into two groups\n10ab + 15b + 4a + 6 GCF on left is5 b, on the right is2 \n5 b(2 a + 3) + 2(2 a + 3) (2 a + 3) is the same! Factor out this GCF\n(2 a + 3)(5 b + 2) Our Solution\nThe key for grouping to work is after the GCF is factored out of the left and\nright, the two binomials must match exactly. If there is any d iﬀerence between\nthe two we either have to do some adjusting or it can’t be facto red using the\ngrouping method. Consider the following example. \nExample 274. \n6x2 + 9xy − 14x − 21 y Split problem into two groups\n6x2 + 9xy\n− 14x − 21 y GCF on left is3x, on right is7\n3x(2 x + 3y) + 7( − 2 x − 3y) The signs in the parenthesis don′t match! \nwhen the signs don’t match on both terms we can easily make the m match by fac-\ntoring the opposite of the GCF on the right side. Instead of 7 we will use − 7. \nThis will change the signs inside the second parenthesis. \n3x(2 x + 3y) − 7(2 x + 3y) (2 x + 3y) is the same! Factor out this GCF\n(2 x + 3y)(3x − 7) Our Solution\nOften we can recognize early that we need to use the opposite o f the GCF when\nfactoring. If the ﬁrst term of the ﬁrst binomial is positive i n the problem, we will\nalso want the ﬁrst term of the second binomial to be positive. If it is negative\nthen we will use the opposite of the GCF to be sure they match. \nExample 275. \n5 xy − 8x − 10y + 16 Split the problem into two groups\n5 xy − 8x\n− 10y + 16 GCF on left isx, on right we needa negative, \nso we use− 2 \nx(5 y − 8) − 2(5 y − 8) (5 y − 8) is the same! Factor out this GCF\n(5 y − 8)( x − 2) Our Solution\n217"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_37"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Grouping well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [
            "Explain the concept of Grouping and its role in factoring polynomials",
            "Use Grouping to factor a polynomial with four terms",
            "Recognize when to use the opposite of the GCF when factoring"
          ],
          "concepts": [

              "id": "SEC_37_C1",
              "title": "What is Grouping?",
              "explanation": "Grouping is a strategy used to factor polynomials with four terms. It involves dividing the polynomial into two parts, finding the greatest common factor (GCF) of each part, and factoring out the GCF from both parts. This leaves us with binomials that can be multiplied together.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_38",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.3",
          "title": "Trinomials where a = 1",
          "order": 37,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 221,
              "page_end": 221,
              "text": "6.3\nFactoring - Trinomials where a = 1 \nObjective: Factor trinomials where the coeﬃcient of x 2 is one. \nFactoring with three terms, or trinomials, is the most impor tant type of factoring\nto be able to master. As factoring is multiplication backwar ds we will start with a\nmultipication problem and look at how we can reverse the proc ess. \nExample 282. \n( x + 6)( x − 4) Distribute ( x + 6) through second parenthesis\nx( x + 6) − 4( x + 6) Distribute each monomial through parenthesis\nx2 + 6x − 4x − 24 Combine like terms\nx2 + 2 x − 24 Our Solution\nYou may notice that if you reverse the last three steps the pro cess looks like\ngrouping. This is because it is grouping! The GCF of the left t wo terms is x and\nthe GCF of the second two terms is − 4. The way we will factor trinomials is to\nmake them into a polynomial with four terms and then factor by grouping. This\nis shown in the following example, the same problem worked ba ckwards\nExample 283. \nx2 + 2 x − 24 Split middle term into + 6x − 4x\nx2 + 6x − 4x − 24 Grouping : GCF on left isx, on right is− 4\nx( x + 6) − 4( x + 6) ( x + 6) is the same, factor out this GCF\n( x + 6)( x − 4) Our Solution\nThe trick to make these problems work is how we split the middl e term. Why did\nwe pick + 6x − 4x and not + 5 x − 3x? The reason is because 6x − 4x is the only\ncombination that works! So how do we know what is the one combi nation that\nworks? To ﬁnd the correct way to split the middle term we will u se what is called\nthe ac method. In the next lesson we will discuss why it is call ed the ac method. \nThe way the ac method works is we ﬁnd a pair of numers that multi ply to a cer-\ntain number and add to another number. Here we will try to mult iply to get the\nlast term and add to get the coeﬃcient of the middle term. In th e previous\n221\nexample that would mean we wanted to multiply to − 24 and add to + 2 . The\nonly numbers that can do this are 6 and − 4 ( 6 · − 4 = − 24 and 6 + ( − 4) = 2) . \nThis process is shown in the next few examples\nExample 284. \nx2 + 9x + 18 Want to multiply to 18 , add to 9\nx2 + 6x + 3x + 18 6 and 3, split the middle term\nx( x + 6) + 3( x + 6) Factor by grouping\n( x + 6)( x + 3) Our Solution\nExample 285. \nx2 − 4x + 3 Want to multiply to3, add to − 4\nx2 − 3x − x + 3 − 3 and − 1 , split the middle term\nx( x − 3) − 1( x − 3) Factor by grouping\n( x − 3)( x − 1) Our Solution\nExample 286. \nx2 − 8x − 20 Want to multiply to − 20, add to − 8\nx2 − 10x + 2 x − 20 − 10 and 2 , split the middle term\nx( x − 10) + 2( x − 10) Factor by grouping\n( x − 10)( x + 2) Our Solution\nOften when factoring we have two variables. These problems s olve just like prob-\nlems with one variable, using the coeﬃcients to decide how to split the middle\nterm\nExample 287. \na2 − 9ab + 14b2 Want to multiply to 14, add to − 9\na2 − 7ab − 2 ab + 14b2 − 7 and − 2 , split the middle term\na( a − 7b) − 2 b( a − 7b) Factor by grouping\n( a − 7b)( a − 2 b) Our Solution\n222"
            }
          ],

              "id": "SEC_38_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why Factoring Trinomials Matters",
              "body": "Factoring trinomials is an important skill to master in algebra because it allows us to simplify complex expressions and solve equations. When we have a trinomial that can be factored, we can use the resulting factors to our advantage. For example, if we have a quadratic expression like x^2 + 6x - 24, we can factor it into (x+6)(x-4). This makes it easier to solve equations and simplify expressions involving this trinomial.",

              "title": "The ac Method for Factoring Trinomials",
              "body": "To factor a trinomial in the form ax^2 + bx + c, where a = 1, we use the ac method. This involves finding two numbers whose product is equal to c and whose sum is equal to b. These two numbers can be used to split the middle term into two terms, which are then grouped with the other two terms in the trinomial.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 221"
              ]

              "id": "SEC_38_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example 1: Factoring x^2 + 9x + 18",
              "body": "To factor this trinomial using the ac method, we need to find a pair of numbers whose product is equal to 18 and whose sum is equal to 9. These numbers are 6 and 3, so we split the middle term into 6x and 3x. Then we group these terms with the other two terms in the trinomial: x(x+6) + 3(x+6). Finally, we factor out the common binomial (x+6): (x+6)(x+3)",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 221"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_38_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistakes when Factoring Trinomials with a Leading Coefficient of 1",
              "body": "One common mistake is to forget to use the ac method and instead try to factor the trinomial by grouping. However, this approach often leads to incorrect factors. Another mistake is to not check if the numbers you've chosen to split the middle term actually multiply to the constant term and add up to the coefficient of the middle term.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 221"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_38_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation: Understanding the ac Method",
              "body": "To make sure we understand the ac method, let's go back to the example x^2 + 9x + 18. We need to find a pair of numbers whose product is equal to 18 and whose sum is equal to 9. These numbers are indeed 6 and 3, so we can split the middle term into 6x and 3x. What's important here is that we're not just looking for any two numbers that multiply to 18 and add up to 9, but rather we're using these numbers to split the middle term in a way that helps us factor the trinomial.",
              "source_refs": [

              ]
            }

              "prompt": "Use the ac method to factor this trinomial: x^2 + 9x + 18",

              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 229,
              "page_end": 229,
              "text": "6.5\nFactoring - Factoring Special Products\nObjective: Identify and factor special products including a diﬀerence of\nsquares, perfect squares, and sum and diﬀerence of cubes. \nWhen factoring there are a few special products that, if we ca n recognize them, \ncan help us factor polynomials. The ﬁrst is one we have seen be fore. When multi-\nplying special products we found that a sum and a diﬀerence co uld multiply to a\ndiﬀerence of squares. Here we will use this special product t o help us factor\nDiﬀerence of Squares: a2 − b2 = ( a + b)( a − b) \nIf we are subtracting two perfect squares then it will always factor to the sum and\ndiﬀerence of the square roots. \nExample 300. \nx2 − 16 Subtracting two perfect squares , the square roots are x and 4\n( x + 4)( x − 4) Our Solution\nExample 301. \n9a2 − 25b2 Subtracting two perfect squares, the square roots are 3a and 5 b\n(3a + 5 b)(3a − 5 b) Our Solution\nIt is important to note, that a sum of squares will never facto r. It is always\nprime. This can be seen if we try to use the ac method to factor x2 + 36. \nExample 302. \nx2 + 36 No bx term, we use 0 x. \nx2 + 0 x + 36 Multiply to 36 , add to 0 \n1 · 36, 2 · 18, 3 · 12, 4 · 9, 6 · 6 No combinations that multiply to 36 add to0 \nPrime, cannot factor Our Solution\n229\nIt turns out that a sum of squares is always prime. \nSum of Squares: a2 + b2 = Prime\nA great example where we see a sum of squares comes from factor ing a diﬀerence\nof 4th powers. Because the square root of a fourth power is a sq uare ( a4\n√\n= a2 ), \nwe can factor a diﬀerence of fourth powers just like we factor a diﬀerence of\nsquares, to a sum and diﬀerence of the square roots. This will give us two factors, \none which will be a prime sum of squares, and a second which wil l be a diﬀerence\nof squares which we can factor again. This is shown in the foll owing examples. \nExample 303. \na4 − b4 Diﬀerence of squares with roots a2 and b2 \n( a2 + b2 )( a2 − b2 ) The ﬁrst factor is prime, the second is a diﬀerence of squares! \n( a2 + b2 )( a + b)( a − b) Our Solution\nExample 304. \nx4 − 16 Diﬀerence of squares with roots x2 and 4\n( x2 + 4)( x2 − 4) The ﬁrst factor is prime, the second is a diﬀerence of squares! \n( x2 + 4)( x + 2)( x − 2) Our Solution\nAnother factoring shortcut is the perfect square. We had a sh ortcut for multi-\nplying a perfect square which can be reversed to help us facto r a perfect square\nPerfect Square: a2 + 2 ab + b2 = ( a + b) 2 \nA perfect square can be diﬃcult to recognize at ﬁrst glance, b ut if we use the ac\nmethod and get two of the same numbers we know we have a perfect square. \nThen we can just factor using the square roots of the ﬁrst and l ast terms and the\nsign from the middle. This is shown in the following examples . \nExample 305. \nx2 − 6x + 9 Multiply to 9, add to − 6\nThe numbers are− 3 and − 3, the same! Perfect square\n( x − 3) 2 Use square roots from ﬁrst and last terms and sign from the middle\n230"
            }

        {
          "id": "SEC_40",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.6",
          "title": "Factoring Strategy",
          "order": 39,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 234,
              "page_end": 234,
              "text": "6.6\nFactoring - Factoring Strategy\nObjective: Idenﬁty and use the correct method to factor vari ous poly-\nnomials. \nWith so many diﬀerent tools used to factor, it is easy to get lo st as to which tool\nto use when. Here we will attempt to organize all the diﬀerent factoring types we\nhave seen. A large part of deciding how to solve a problem is ba sed on how many\nterms are in the problem. For all problem types we will always try to factor out\nthe GCF ﬁrst. \nFactoring Strategy (GCF First!!!!!) \n• 2 terms: sum or diﬀerence of squares or cubes: \na2 − b2 = ( a + b)( a − b) \na2 + b2 = Prime\na3 + b3 = ( a + b)( a2 − ab + b2 ) \na3 − b3 = ( a − b)( a2 + ab + b2 ) \n• 3 terms: ac method, watch for perfect square! \na2 + 2 ab + b2 = ( a + b) 2 \nMultiply to ac and add to b\n• 4 terms: grouping\nWe will use the above strategy to factor each of the following examples. Here the\nemphasis will be on which strategy to use rather than the step s used in that\nmethod. \nExample 312. \n4x2 + 56xy + 196y2 GCF ﬁrst, 4\n4( x2 + 14xy + 49y2 ) Three terms, try ac method, multiply to 49, add to 14\n7 and 7, perfect square! \n234\n4( x + 7y) 2 Our Solution\nExample 313. \n5 x 2 y + 15xy − 35x2 − 105x GCF ﬁrst, 5 x\n5 x( xy + 3y − 7x − 21 ) Four terms, try grouping\n5 x[ y( x + 3) − 7( x + 3)] ( x + 3) match! \n5 x( x + 3)( y − 7) Our Solution\nExample 314. \n100x2 − 400 GCF ﬁrst , 100\n100( x2 − 4) Two terms, diﬀerence of squares\n100( x + 4)( x − 4) Our Solution\nExample 315. \n108x3y2 − 39x2 y2 + 3xy 2 GCF ﬁrst, 3xy 2 \n3xy 2 ( 36x2 − 13x + 1) Thee terms, ac method, multiply to 36, add to − 13\n3xy 2 ( 36x2 − 9x − 4x + 1) − 9 and − 4, split middle term\n3xy 2 [9x(4x − 1) − 1(4x − 1)] Factor by grouping\n3xy 2 (4x − 1)(9x − 1) Our Solution\nWorld View Note: Variables originated in ancient Greece where Aristotle wou ld\nuse a single capital letter to represent a number. \nExample 316. \n5 + 625y3 GCF ﬁrst, 5 \n5(1 + 125y3) Two terms, sum of cubes\n5(1 + 5 y)(1 − 5 y + 25y2 ) Our Solution\nIt is important to be comfortable and conﬁdent not just with u sing all the fac-\ntoring methods, but decided on which method to use. This is wh y practice is very\nimportant! \n235"
            }
          ],

              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why We Need Factoring Strategy",
              "body": "In algebra, we often encounter polynomials with multiple terms. Without a clear strategy, it's easy to get lost in the various methods for factoring. Factoring Strategy provides a framework for approaching these problems systematically and efficiently. By mastering this approach, you'll become more confident and accurate in your factoring skills.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 234"

              "id": "SEC_40_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Factoring Strategy: A Step-by-Step Approach",
              "body": "The Factoring Strategy involves the following steps: (1) Identify the number of terms in the polynomial. (2) Select the appropriate method based on the number of terms and the type of polynomial (GCF First, AC method, or grouping). (3) Apply the chosen method to factor the polynomial.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 234"

              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Factoring a Trinomial using the AC Method",
              "body": "Let's factor the trinomial x^2 + 6xy + y^2. To do this, we'll follow the AC method: (1) Multiply to ac and add to b: (x)(y^2) + (6x)(y) = xy^2 + 6xy. (2) Factor out the common binomial factor (xy + 3y). (3) Factor the remaining expression into (x + 3y)(y).",
              "source_refs": [

              ]

              "title": "Common Pitfall: Overlooking GCF",

        {
          "id": "SEC_41",
          "chapter_id": "CH_7",
          "number": "6.7",
          "title": "Solve by Factoring",
          "order": 40,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 237,
              "page_end": 237,
              "text": "6.7\nFactoring - Solve by Factoring\nObjective: Solve quadratic equation by factoring and using the zero\nproduct rule. \nWhen solving linear equations such as 2 x − 5 = 21 we can solve for the variable\ndirectly by adding 5 and dividing by 2 to get 13. However, when we have x2 (or a\nhigher power of x) we cannot just isolate the variable as we did with the linear\nequations. One method that we can use to solve for the varaibl e is known as the\nzero product rule\nZero Product Rule: If a b= 0 then either a = 0 or b = 0\nThe zero product rule tells us that if two factors are multipl ied together and the\nanswer is zero, then one of the factors must be zero. We can use this to help us\nsolve factored polynomials as in the following example. \nExample 317. \n(2 x − 3)(5 x + 1) = 0 One factor must be zero\n2 x − 3 = 0 or 5 x + 1 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 3 + 3\n− 1 − 1 Solve each equation\n2 x = 3 or 5 x = − 1 \n2 2 5 5 \nx = 3\n2 \nor − 1 \n5 Our Solution\nFor the zero product rule to work we must have factors to set eq ual to zero. This\nmeans if the problem is not already factored we will factor it ﬁrst. \nExample 318. \n4x2 + x − 3 = 0 Factor using the ac method, multiply to − 12, add to 1 \n4x2 − 3x + 4x − 3 = 0 The numbers are− 3 and 4, split the middle term\nx(4x − 3) + 1(4x − 3) = 0 Factor by grouping\n(4x − 3)( x + 1) = 0 One factor must be zero\n4x − 3 = 0 or x + 1 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n237\n+ 3 + 3 − 1 − 1 Solve each equation\n4x = 3 or x = − 1 \n4 4\nx = 3\n4\nor − 1 Our Solution\nAnother important part of the zero product rule is that befor e we factor, the\nequation must equal zero. If it does not, we must move terms ar ound so it does\nequal zero. Generally we like the x2 term to be positive. \nExample 319. \nx2 = 8x − 15 Set equal to zero by moving terms to the left\n− 8x + 15 − 8x + 15\nx2 − 8x + 15 = 0 Factor using the ac method, multiply to 15, add to − 8\n( x − 5)( x − 3) = 0 The numbers are− 5 and − 3\nx − 5 = 0 or x − 3 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 5 + 5 + 3 + 3 Solve each equation\nx = 5 or x = 3 Our Solution\nExample 320. \n( x − 7)( x + 3) = − 9 Not equal to zero, multiply ﬁrst, use FOIL\nx2 − 7x + 3x − 21 = − 9 Combine like terms\nx2 − 4x − 21 = − 9 Move − 9 to other side so equation equals zero\n+ 9 + 9\nx2 − 4x − 12 = 0 Factor using the ac method, mutiply to − 12, add to − 4\n( x − 6)( x + 2) = 0 The numbers are6 and − 2 \nx − 6 = 0 or x + 2 = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n+ 6 + 6 − 2 − 2 Solve each equation\nx = 6 or − 2 Our Solution\nExample 321. \n3x2 + 4x − 5 = 7x2 + 4x − 14 Set equal to zero by moving terms to the right\n− 3x2 − 4x + 5 − 3x2 − 4x + 5 \n0 = 4x2 − 9 Factor using diﬀerence of squares\n238"
            }
          ],

              "id": "SEC_41_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Solve by Factoring: Understanding the Idea",
              "body": "When we encounter quadratic equations, we often can't just isolate the variable like we do with linear equations. However, if we have a factored polynomial, we can use the Zero Product Rule to solve for x. The idea is that one of the factors must be zero, so we set each factor equal to zero and solve for x.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 237"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_41_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "The Zero Product Rule",
              "body": "If a * b = 0, then either a = 0 or b = 0. This means that if two factors are multiplied together and equal zero, one of the factors must be zero.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 237"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_41_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solving (2x - 3)(5x + 1) = 0",
              "body": "Step 1: We have a factored polynomial (2x - 3)(5x + 1) = 0. Since it's equal to zero, we can use the Zero Product Rule.Step 2: Set each factor equal to zero: 2x - 3 = 0 or 5x + 1 = 0.Step 3: Solve each equation separately: 2x = 3 or 5x = -1. This gives us x = 3/2 or x = -1/5.",
              "source_refs": [

      "id": "CH_8",

      "title": "Rational Expressions",
      "order": 7,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_42",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.1",
          "title": "Reduce Rational Expressions",
          "order": 41,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 243,
              "page_end": 243,
              "text": "7.1 \nRational Expressions - Reduce Rational Expressions\nObjective: Reduce rational expressions by dividing out com mon fac-\ntors. \nRational expressions are expressions written as a quotient of polynomials. \nExamples of rational expressions include: \nx2 − x − 12\nx2 − 9x + 20\nand 3\nx − 2 and a − b\nb − a and 3\n2 \nAs rational expressions are a special type of fraction, it is important to remember\nwith fractions we cannot have zero in the denominator of a fra ction. For this\nreason, rational expressions may have one more excluded val ues, or values that\nthe variable cannot be or the expression would be undeﬁned. \nExample 326. \nState the excluded value( s ): x2 − 1 \n3x2 + 5 x\nDenominator cannot be zero\n3x2 + 5 x /\u0002 0 Factor\nx(3x + 5) /\u0002 0 Set each factor not equal to zero\nx /\u0002 0 or 3x + 5 /\u0002 0 Subtract 5 from second equation\n− 5 − 5 \n3x /\u0002 − 5 Divide by 3\n3 3\nx /\u0002\n− 5 \n3 Second equation is solved\nx /\u0002 0 or − 5 \n3 Our Solution\nThis means we can use any value for x in the equation except for 0 and − 5\n3 . We\n243\ncan however, evaluate any other value in the expression. \nWorld View Note: The number zero was not widely accepted in mathematical\nthought around the world for many years. It was the Mayans of C entral America\nwho ﬁrst used zero to aid in the use of their base 20 system as a p lace holder! \nRational expressions are easily evaluated by simply substi tuting the value for the\nvariable and using order of operations. \nExample 327. \nx2 − 4\nx2 + 6x + 8\nwhen x = − 6 Substitute − 5 in for each variable\n( − 6) 2 − 4\n( − 6) 2 + 6( − 6) + 8 Exponents ﬁrst\n36 − 4\n36 + 6( − 6) + 8 Multiply\n36 − 4\n36 − 36 + 8 Add and subtract\n32\n8 Reduce\n4 Our Solution\nJust as we reduced the previous example, often a rational exp ression can be\nreduced, even without knowing the value of the variable. Whe n we reduce we\ndivide out common factors. We have already seen this with mon omials when we\ndiscussed properties of exponents. If the problem only has m onomials we can\nreduce the coeﬃcients, and subtract exponents on the variab les. \nExample 328. \n15x4y2 \n25x2 y6 Reduce , subtract exponents. Negative exponents move to denominator\n3x2 \n5 y4 Our Solution\n244"
            }
          ],

This means that there may be excluded values, or values that the variable cannot be, or the expression would be undefined.",

              "id": "SEC_42_C2",

              "explanation": "Reducing rational expressions involves dividing out common factors, just like we do with monomials when discussing properties of exponents. We can reduce the coefficients and subtract exponents on the variables to simplify the expression.",

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_42_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Why Reduce Rational Expressions?",
              "body": "Reducing rational expressions is an essential skill in algebra, as it allows us to simplify complex fractions and make them easier to work with. It's a fundamental concept that will be used throughout the course, so understanding it now will save you time and effort later on.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 243"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_42_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Reducing Rational Expressions",
              "body": "Reducing rational expressions involves dividing out common factors between the numerator and denominator. This is done by finding the greatest common factor (GCF) of the two polynomials and canceling it out, leaving a simplified fraction.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 243"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_42_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example: Reducing a Rational Expression",
              "body": "Let's reduce the rational expression x^2 - x - 12 / (x + 4). First, we need to find the greatest common factor of the numerator and denominator. In this case, it's (x-3). We can then cancel out the GCF, leaving us with (x-3) / ((x+4)/(x-3)). Simplifying further, we get (x-3)^2 / (x+4)(x-3). Canceling out the common factor again, we're left with x - 3 / (x + 4).",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 243"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_42_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Not Checking for Excluded Values",
              "body": "One common mistake when reducing rational expressions is to forget to check for excluded values. Remember, a rational expression can only be undefined if the denominator is zero. Make sure to identify any excluded values and keep them in mind when simplifying the fraction.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 243"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_42_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation: Reducing Rational Expressions",
              "body": "Think about a time when you reduced a rational expression by canceling out common factors. How did it simplify the fraction? What would happen if you forgot to check for excluded values? Reflect on how reducing rational expressions can help you solve problems more easily.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 243"
              ]
            }
          ],
          "worked_examples": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_42_WE1",
              "title": "Reducing a Rational Expression",

                "Find the greatest common factor of the numerator and denominator: (x-3)",
                "Cancel out the GCF, leaving us with (x-3) / ((x+4)/(x-3))",

              ]

        {
          "id": "SEC_43",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.2",
          "title": "Multiply and Divide",
          "order": 42,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 248,
              "page_end": 248,
              "text": "7.2\nRational Expressions - Multiply & Divide\nObjective: Multiply and divide rational expressions. \nMultiplying and dividing rational expressions is very simi lar to the process we use\nto multiply and divide fractions. \nExample 332. \n15\n49\n· 14\n45\nFirst reduce common factors from numerator and denominator( 15 and 7) \n1 \n7\n· 2 \n3\nMultiply numerators across and denominators across\n2 \n21 Our Solution\nThe process is identical for division with the extra ﬁrst ste p of multiplying by the\nreciprocal. When multiplying with rational expressions we follow the same pro-\ncess, ﬁrst divide out common factors, then multiply straigh t across. \n248\nExample 333. \n25x2 \n9y8 · 24y4\n55x7\nReduce coeﬃcients by dividing out common factors (3 and 5) \nReduce, subtracting exponents, negative exponents in denominator\n5 \n3y4 · 8\n11 x5 Multiply across\n40\n33x5y4 Our Solution\nDivision is identical in process with the extra ﬁrst step of m ultiplying by the\nreciprocal. \nExample 334. \na4b2 \na\n÷ b4\n4 Multiply by the reciprocal\na4b2 \na · 4\nb4 Subtract exponents on variables, negative exponents in denominator\na3\n1 · 4\nb2 Multiply across\n4a3\nb2 Our Solution\nJust as with reducing rational expressions, before we reduc e a multiplication\nproblem, it must be factored ﬁrst. \nExample 335. \nx2 − 9\nx2 + x − 20\n· x2 − 8x + 16\n3x + 9 Factor each numerator and denominator\n( x + 3)( x − 3) \n( x − 4)( x + 5) · ( x − 4)( x − 4) \n3( x + 3) Divide out common factors( x + 3) and ( x − 4) \nx − 3\nx + 5 · x − 4\n3 Multiply across\n249"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_43"
          ],

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_43_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "The Importance of Multiply and Divide in Math",
              "body": "In mathematics, we often encounter complex fractions that involve variables or polynomial expressions. To simplify these fractions, we need to multiply or divide them by other rational expressions. This process is essential for solving equations and manipulating algebraic expressions, which is why understanding how to multiply and divide rational expressions is crucial in math.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 248"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_43_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Rules for Multiplying and Dividing Rational Expressions",
              "body": "When multiplying or dividing rational expressions, we first reduce common factors between the numerator and denominator. Then, we multiply the numerators together and the denominators together. For division, we multiply by the reciprocal of the divisor. This process can be applied to both polynomials and variables, making it a powerful tool for simplifying complex fractions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 248"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_43_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Simplifying Rational Expressions Using Multiply and Divide",
              "body": "Let's say we have the rational expression: (3x^2 / 4) * (5x^2 / 9). To simplify this, we first reduce common factors between the numerator and denominator, which gives us: x^2 / 12. Next, we multiply the numerators together and the denominators together to get: 15x^4 / 108. This is our simplified expression.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 248"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_43_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Pitfalls in Multiplying and Dividing Rational Expressions",
              "body": "A common mistake students make when multiplying or dividing rational expressions is forgetting to reduce common factors between the numerator and denominator before performing the operation. For example, if we have the expression: (x^2 - 4) / (x + 2), we should first factor the numerator to get: ((x-2)(x+2)) / (x+2). Then, we can cancel out common factors between the numerator and denominator to simplify the expression.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 248"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_43_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Understanding the Multiply and Divide Process",
              "body": "To ensure that you understand how to multiply and divide rational expressions, try explaining it in your own words. What are the key steps involved? How do you reduce common factors between the numerator and denominator? What about multiplying by reciprocals for division?",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 248"
              ]
            }

          "id": "SEC_44",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.3",
          "title": "Least Common Denominator",
          "order": 43,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 253,
              "page_end": 253,
              "text": "7.3\nRational Expressions - Least Common Denominators\nObjective: Idenﬁty the least common denominator and build u p\ndenominators to match this common denominator. \nAs with fractions, the least common denominator or LCD is ver y important to\nworking with rational expressions. The process we use to ﬁnd the LCD is based\non the process used to ﬁnd the LCD of intergers. \nExample 338. \nFind the LCD of8 and 6 Consider multiples of the larger number\n8, 16, 24\n/ . 24 is the ﬁrst multiple of8 that is also divisible by6\n24 Our Solution\nWhen ﬁnding the LCD of several monomials we ﬁrst ﬁnd the LCD of the coeﬃ-\ncients, then use all variables and attach the highest expone nt on each variable. \nExample 339. \nFind the LCD of4x2 y5 and 6x4y3z6\nFirst ﬁnd the LCD of coeﬃcients 4 and 6\n12 12 is the LCD of 4 and 6\nx4y5z6 Use all variables with highest exponents on each variable\n12x4y5z6 Our Solution\nThe same pattern can be used on polynomials that have more tha n one term. \nHowever, we must ﬁrst factor each polynomial so we can identi fy all the factors to\nbe used (attaching highest exponent if necessary). \nExample 340. \nFind the LCD of x2 + 2 x − 3 and x2 − x − 12 Factor each polynomial\n( x − 1)( x + 3) and ( x − 4)( x + 3) LCD uses all unique factors\n( x − 1)( x + 3)( x − 4) Our Solution\nNotice we only used ( x + 3) once in our LCD. This is because it only appears as a\nfactor once in either polynomial. The only time we need to rep eat a factor or use\nan exponent on a factor is if there are exponents when one of th e polynomials is\nfactored\n253\nExample 341. \nFind the LCD of x2 − 10x + 25 and x2 − 14x + 45\nFactor each polynomial\n( x − 5) 2 and ( x − 5)( x − 9) LCD uses all unique factors with highest exponent\n( x − 5) 2 ( x − 9) Our Solution\nThe previous example could have also been done with factorin g the ﬁrst polyno-\nmial to ( x − 5)( x − 5) . Then we would have used ( x − 5) twice in the LCD\nbecause it showed up twice in one of the polynomials. However , it is the author’s\nsuggestion to use the exponents in factored form so as to use t he same pattern\n(highest exponent) as used with monomials. \nOnce we know the LCD, our goal will be to build up fractions so t hey have\nmatching denominators. In this lesson we will not be adding a nd subtracting frac-\ntions, just building them up to a common denominator. We can b uild up a frac-\ntion’s denominator by multipliplying the numerator and den oinator by any factors\nthat are not already in the denominator. \nExample 342. \n5 a\n3a2 b\n= ? \n6a5b3 Idenﬁty what factors we need to match denominators\n2 a3b2 3 · 2 = 6and we need three morea′s and two moreb′s \n5 a\n3a2 b\n( 2 a3b2 \n2 a3b2 \n)\nMultiply numerator and denominator by this\n10a4b2 \n6a5b3 Our Solution\nExample 343. \nx − 2 \nx + 4 = ? \nx2 + 7x + 12 Factor to idenﬁty factors we need to match denominators\n( x + 4)( x + 3) \n( x + 3) The missing factor\nx − 2 \nx + 4\n( x + 3\nx + 3\n)\nMultiply numerator and denominator by missing factor, \nFOIL numerator\nx2 + x − 6\n( x + 4)( x + 3) Our Solution\n254"
            }
          ],

            "COMP_44"

            {
              "id": "SEC_44_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Introduction to Least Common Denominator",
              "body": "In mathematics, rational expressions are a powerful tool for modeling real-world situations. However, working with fractions can be challenging when the denominators are different. This is where the concept of Least Common Denominator (LCD) comes in. The LCD is a fundamental concept that allows us to combine fractions with different denominators, making it easier to solve problems and simplify expressions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 253"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_44_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Least Common Denominator",
              "body": "The Least Common Denominator (LCD) of two or more fractions is the smallest multiple that all denominators can divide into evenly. It's denoted by a variable, usually 'd', and is used to rewrite fractions with different denominators as equivalent fractions with the same denominator.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 253"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_44_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Finding the LCD of Monomials",
              "body": "Find the LCD of 8x^2 and 6x. First, identify the greatest common factor (GCF) of the coefficients, which is 2. Then, use all variables and attach the highest exponent on each variable: x^2 = x^2. The LCD is therefore 2x^2.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 253"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_44_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Using the Wrong Exponent",
              "body": "One common mistake when finding the LCD of polynomials is to use the wrong exponent on a factor. For example, if we have x^2 + 3x and x^2 - 4x, we might mistakenly attach an exponent of 1 instead of 0 to the x term. This can lead to incorrect results.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 253"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_44_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "Self-Explanation: Understanding LCD",
              "body": "Now that you've learned about Least Common Denominator, take a moment to reflect on what it means and how it applies to real-world problems. Think about a situation where you might use the LCD to combine fractions with different denominators.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 253"
              ]
            }
          ],

                "Step 1: Identify the greatest common factor (GCF) of the coefficients, which is 2.",

        {
          "id": "SEC_45",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.4",
          "title": "Add and Subtract",
          "order": 44,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 257,
              "page_end": 257,
              "text": "7.4\nRational Expressions - Add & Subtract\nObjective: Add and subtract rational expressions with and w ithout\ncommon denominators. \nAdding and subtracting rational expressions is identical t o adding and subtracting\nwith integers. Recall that when adding with a common denomin ator we add the\nnumerators and keep the denominator. This is the same proces s used with\nrational expressions. Remember to reduce, if possible, you r ﬁnal answer. \nExample 346. \nx − 4\nx2 − 2 x − 8\n+ x + 8\nx2 − 2 x − 8 Same denominator, add numerators, combine like terms\n2 x + 4\nx2 − 2 x − 8 Factor numerator and denominator\n2( x + 2) \n( x + 2)( x − 4) Divide out ( x + 2) \n2 \nx − 4 Our Solution\n257\nSubtraction with common denominator follows the same patte rn, though the sub-\ntraction can cause problems if we are not careful with it. To a void sign errors we\nwill ﬁrst distribute the subtraction through the numerator . Then we can treat it\nlike an addition problem. This process is the same as “add the opposite” we saw\nwhen subtracting with negatives. \nExample 347. \n6x − 12\n3x − 6\n− 15x − 6\n3x − 6 Add the opposite of the second fraction( distribute negative) \n6x − 12\n3x − 6 + − 15x + 6\n3x − 6 Add numerators, combine like terms\n− 9x − 6\n3x − 6 Factor numerator and denominator\n− 3(3x + 2) \n3( x − 2) Divide out common factor of3\n− (3x + 2) \nx − 2 Our Solution\nWorld View Note: The Rhind papyrus of Egypt from 1650 BC gives some of\nthe earliest known symbols for addition and subtraction, a p air of legs walking in\nthe direction one reads for addition, and a pair of legs walki ng in the opposite\ndirection for subtraction.. \nWhen we don’t have a common denominator we will have to ﬁnd the least\ncommon denominator (LCD) and build up each fraction so the de nominators\nmatch. The following example shows this process with intege rs. \nExample 348. \n5 \n6\n+ 1 \n4\nThe LCD is 12. Build up, multiply 6 by 2 and 4 by 3\n( 2 \n2 \n) 5 \n6\n+ 1 \n4\n( 3\n3\n)\nMultiply\n10\n12\n+ 3\n12 Add numerators\n258"
            }
          ],

If we already have a common denominator, we simply add the numerators and keep the denominator. This process is similar to adding fractions with integers.",

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_45_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "The Importance of Adding and Subtracting Rational Expressions",
              "body": "Adding and subtracting rational expressions is a fundamental concept in algebra that allows us to simplify complex fractions. It's essential to understand this process, as it will be used extensively throughout the course. Think of it like combining two different amounts: if you have $5 in one pocket and your friend has $3 in another, how would you add them together? Similarly, when adding or subtracting rational expressions, we need to find a common ground, or denominator, to combine them correctly.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 257"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_45_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "Definition of Adding and Subtracting Rational Expressions",
              "body": "Adding rational expressions: When adding two rational expressions with a common denominator, we add the numerators and keep the denominator. For example, if we have (x - 4) / (x^2 - 2x - 8) + x + 8 / (x^2 - 2x - 8), we can add the numerators (x - 4 and x + 8) to get 2x + 4, while keeping the denominator (x^2 - 2x - 8). Subtracting rational expressions: When subtracting two rational expressions with a common denominator, we first distribute the subtraction through the numerator, then treat it like an addition problem by finding a common denominator and adding the numerators. For example, if we have (6x - 12) / (3x - 6) - (15x + 6) / (3x - 6), we can first distribute the subtraction through the numerator to get -(15x + 6), then treat it like an addition problem by finding a common denominator and adding the numerators.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 257"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_45_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Example: Adding Rational Expressions with Common Denominators",
              "body": "Let's consider the example from the textbook (x - 4) / (x^2 - 2x - 8) + x + 8 / (x^2 - 2x - 8). To add these two rational expressions, we first need to find a common denominator, which is already given as (x^2 - 2x - 8). Next, we add the numerators: (x - 4) + (x + 8) = 2x + 4. Now, our result is a single rational expression with the numerator 2x + 4 and denominator (x^2 - 2x - 8). To simplify this expression, we can try to factor the numerator or denominator. In this case, the numerator 2x + 4 factors as 2(x + 2), while the denominator x^2 - 2x - 8 can be factored as (x + 2)(x - 4). Since both have a common factor of (x + 2), we can divide out this common factor to get our final result.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 257"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_45_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Mistake: Not Distributing the Subtraction through the Numerator",
              "body": "When subtracting rational expressions, it's essential to distribute the subtraction through the numerator. If we forget to do this, we may end up with incorrect results. For example, if we have (6x - 12) / (3x - 6) - (15x + 6) / (3x - 6), without distributing the subtraction, we would simply subtract the numerators, getting -(21x - 18). However, this is incorrect! We need to distribute the subtraction through the numerator first. Once we do that, we can treat it like an addition problem by finding a common denominator and adding the numerators.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 257"
              ]
            },
            {

              "kind": "self_explanation",

Think about a time when you might use the addition or subtraction of rational expressions in real life (e.g., combining amounts, simplifying fractions). Write down one thing that you're still unsure about and one question you have for your instructor.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 257"
              ]
            }

        {
          "id": "SEC_46",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.5",
          "title": "Complex Fractions",
          "order": 45,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 262,
              "page_end": 262,
              "text": "7.5\nRational Expressions - Complex Fractions\nObjective: Simplify complex fractions by multiplying each term by the\nleast common denominator. \nComplex fractions have fractions in either the numerator, o r denominator, or usu-\nally both. These fractions can be simpliﬁed in one of two ways . This will be illus-\ntrated ﬁrst with integers, then we will consider how the proc ess can be expanded\nto include expressions with variables. \nThe ﬁrst method uses order of operations to simplify the nume rator and denomi-\nnator ﬁrst, then divide the two resulting fractions by multi plying by the recip-\nrocal. \nExample 353. \n2 \n3\n− 1 \n4\n5\n6\n+ 1 \n2 \nGet common denominator in top and bottom fractions\n8\n12 − 3\n12 \n5\n6\n+ 3\n6\nAdd and subtract fractions, reducing solutions\n5\n12 \n4\n3\nTo divide fractions we multiply by the reciprocal\n( 5 \n12\n)( 3\n4\n)\nReduce\n( 5 \n4\n)( 1 \n4\n)\nMultiply\n5 \n16 Our Solution\nThe process above works just ﬁne to simplify, but between get ting common\ndenominators, taking reciprocals, and reducing, it can be a very involved process. \nGenerally we prefer a diﬀerent method, to multiply the numer ator and denomi-\nnator of the large fraction (in eﬀect each term in the complex fraction) by the\nleast common denominator (LCD). This will allow us to reduce and clear the\nsmall fractions. We will simplify the same problem using thi s second method. \nExample 354. \n2 \n3\n− 1 \n4\n5\n6\n+ 1 \n2 \nLCD is 12, multiply each term\n262\n2( 12 ) \n3 − 1( 12 ) \n4\n5( 12 ) \n6 + 1( 12 ) \n2 \nReduce each fraction\n2(4) − 1(3) \n5(2) + 1(6) Multiply\n8 − 3\n10 + 6 Add and subtract\n5 \n16 Our Solution\nClearly the second method is a much cleaner and faster method to arrive at our\nsolution. It is the method we will use when simplifying with v ariables as well. We\nwill ﬁrst ﬁnd the LCD of the small fractions, and multiply eac h term by this LCD\nso we can clear the small fractions and simplify. \nExample 355. \n1 − 1 \nx2 \n1 − 1 \nx\nIdentify LCD ( use highest exponent) \nLCD = x2 Multiply each term by LCD\n1( x2 ) − 1( x2 ) \nx2 \n1( x2 ) − 1( x2 ) \nx\nReduce fractions ( subtract exponents) \n1( x2 ) − 1 \n1( x2 ) − x Multiply\nx2 − 1 \nx2 − x Factor\n( x + 1)( x − 1) \nx( x − 1) Divide out ( x − 1) factor\nx + 1 \nx Our Solution\nThe process is the same if the LCD is a binomial, we will need to distribute\n3\nx + 4 − 2 \n5 + 2 \nx + 4\nMultiply each term by LCD, ( x + 4) \n263"
            }
          ],

            "Recognize when to use the second method for simplifying complex fractions"

              "id": "SEC_46_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "Introduction to Complex Fractions",
              "body": "When we encounter complex fractions in our daily lives or in mathematics, it's essential to simplify them as much as possible.               "kind": "definition",

              "body": "A complex fraction is defined as a fraction that contains one or more fractions in its numerator, denominator, or both.               "source_refs": [

              "title": "Example 1: Simplifying a Complex Fraction with Integers",
              "body": "Let's simplify the following complex fraction: \\frac{2}{3} - \\frac{1}{4}. First, we find the least common denominator (LCD), which is 12. Then, we multiply each term by the LCD: 2(12) / 3(12) - 1(12) / 4(12). Next, we reduce each fraction: 8 / 9 - 3 / 12. Finally, we add and subtract: 5 / 36.",
              "source_refs": [

              "title": "Common Mistake: Finding a Common Denominator Instead of the Least Common Denominator",
              "body": "One common mistake students make when simplifying complex fractions is finding a common denominator instead of the least common denominator. This can lead to unnecessary complications and incorrect results. To avoid this, remember that the LCD is the smallest multiple of all the denominators in a complex fraction.",

              "title": "Example 2: Simplifying a Complex Fraction with Variables",
              "prompt": "Simplify the following complex fraction: \\frac{1}{x^2} - \\frac{1}{x}.",
              "steps": [
                "Solve Step 1: Identify the least common denominator (LCD). The LCD of x^2 and x is x^2.",

                "Solve Step 3: Reduce each fraction: x^2 / x^4 - x^2 / x^3.",
                "Solve Step 4: Add and subtract: (x^2 - x^2) / x^3 = 0 / x^3."

          "id": "SEC_47",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.6",
          "title": "Proportions",
          "order": 46,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 268,
              "page_end": 268,
              "text": "7.6\nRational Expressions - Proportions\nObjective: Solve proportions using the cross product and us e propor-\ntions to solve application problems\nWhen two fractions are equal, they are called a proportion. T his deﬁnition can be\ngeneralized to two equal rational expressions. Proportion s have an important\nproperty called the cross-product. \nCross Product: If a\nb\n= c\nd then ad = bc\nThe cross product tells us we can multiply diagonally to get a n equation with no\nfractions that we can solve. \nExample 359. \n20\n6\n= x\n9 Calculate cross product\n268\n( 20)(9) = 6x Multiply\n180 = 6x Divide both sides by6\n6 6\n30 = x Our Solution\nWorld View Note: The ﬁrst clear deﬁnition of a proportion and the notation\nfor a proportion came from the German Leibniz who wrote, “I wr ite dy : x = dt: a; \nfor dy is to x as dt is to a, is indeed the same as, dy divided by x is equal to dt\ndivided by a. From this equation follow then all the rules of proportion. ”\nIf the proportion has more than one term in either numerator o r denominator, we\nwill have to distribute while calculating the cross product . \nExample 360. \nx + 3\n4 = 2 \n5 \nCalculate cross product\n5( x + 3) = (4)(2) Multiply and distribute\n5 x + 15 = 8 Solve\n− 15 − 15 Subtract 15 from both sides\n5 x = − 7 Divide both sides by5 \n5 5 \nx = − 7\n5 \nOur Solution\nThis same idea can be seen when the variable appears in severa l parts of the pro-\nportion. \nExample 361. \n4\nx\n= 6\n3x + 2 Calculate cross product\n4(3x + 2) = 6x Distribute\n12x + 8 = 6x Move variables to one side\n− 12x − 12x Subtract 12x from both sides\n8 = − 6x Divide both sides by− 6\n− 6 − 6\n− 4\n3\n= x Our Solution\n269"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_47"
          ],

          ],

              "id": "SEC_47_C1",
              "title": "What is a Proportion?",
              "explanation": "A proportion is an equation between two equal rational expressions. It can be thought of as two fractions being equal, or more generally, two equal ratios. For example, if we have the ratio of 20 to 6 being equal to some unknown number x, then we can write this as a proportion: (20/6) = (x/9).",
              "source_refs": [

              "id": "SEC_47_C2",
              "title": "The Cross-Product Property",
              "explanation": "A fundamental property of proportions is the cross-product. If two fractions are equal, then their cross-products must also be equal. In other words, if a/b = c/d, then ad = bc. This means that we can multiply diagonally to eliminate any fractions and solve for unknowns.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 268"
              ],

          "lesson_blocks": [
            {
              "id": "SEC_47_B1",
              "kind": "teaching",
              "title": "What is the Importance of Proportions?",
              "body": "Proportions are a fundamental concept in mathematics, and they have numerous applications in various fields such as engineering, physics, and finance. They help us describe relationships between quantities and make predictions based on those relationships. For instance, if we know that a certain machine can produce 20 units of output for every 6 units of input, then we can use this proportion to calculate how much output we can expect from a given amount of input.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 268"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_47_B2",
              "kind": "definition",
              "title": "What is the Cross-Product Property?",
              "body": "The cross-product property states that if two fractions are equal, then their cross-products must also be equal. In other words, if a/b = c/d, then ad = bc. This means that we can multiply diagonally to eliminate any fractions and solve for unknowns. Note that this property only holds when the fractions are equal, so it's essential to verify this before applying the cross-product method.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 268"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_47_B3",
              "kind": "worked_example",
              "title": "Solving a Proportion with Two Equal Rational Expressions",
              "body": "Let's say we want to solve the proportion: (x + 3)/4 = 2/5. To apply the cross-product property, we multiply diagonally: (x + 3) × 5 = 4 × 2. This gives us: 5x + 15 = 8. Next, we isolate x by subtracting 15 from both sides and then dividing by 5: 5x = -7, so x = -7/5.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 268"
              ]
            },
            {
              "id": "SEC_47_B4",
              "kind": "common_mistake",
              "title": "Common Error: Assuming the Cross-Product Property Always Holds",
              "body": "One common mistake students make is assuming that the cross-product property always holds. However, this property only applies when the fractions are equal. If we try to apply it without verifying equality first, we may end up with incorrect results. For instance, in our previous example, if we had multiplied diagonally without checking if the fractions were indeed equal, we would have obtained a different result.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 268"
              ]
            },

              "id": "SEC_47_B5",
              "kind": "self_explanation",
              "title": "What Did I Learn About Proportions?",
              "body": "I learned that proportions are equations between two equal rational expressions. They have a fundamental property called the cross-product, which allows us to multiply diagonally and eliminate fractions. By applying this property correctly, we can solve proportions with two equal rational expressions.",
              "source_refs": [
                "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf p. 268"
              ]
            }

        {
          "id": "SEC_48",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.7",
          "title": "Solving Rational Equations",
          "order": 47,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 274,
              "page_end": 274,
              "text": "7.7\nRational Expressions - Solving Rational Equations\nObjective: Solve rational equations by identifying and mul tiplying by\nthe least common denominator. \nWhen solving equations that are made up of rational expressi ons we will solve\nthem using the same strategy we used to solve linear equation s with fractions. \nWhen we solved problems like the next example, we cleared the fraction by multi-\nplying by the least common denominator (LCD) \nExample 366. \n2 \n3\nx − 5 \n6\n= 3\n4\nMultiply each term by LCD, 12\n2( 12) \n3 x − 5( 12) \n6 = 3( 12) \n4 Reduce fractions\n2(4) x − 5(2) = 3(3) Multiply\n8x − 10 = 9 Solve\n+ 10 + 10 Add 10 to both sides\n8x = 19 Divide both sides by 8\n8 8\nx = 19\n8 Our Solution\nWe will use the same process to solve rational equations, the only diﬀerence is our\n274\nLCD will be more involved. We will also have to be aware of doma in issues. If our\nLCD equals zero, the solution is undeﬁned. We will always che ck our solutions in\nthe LCD as we may have to remove a solution from our solution se t. \nExample 367. \n5 x + 5 \nx + 2 + 3x = x2 \nx + 2 Multiply each term by LCD, ( x + 2) \n(5 x + 5)( x + 2) \nx + 2 + 3x( x + 2) = x2 ( x + 2) \nx + 2 Reduce fractions\n5 x + 5 + 3x( x + 2) = x2 Distribute\n5 x + 5 + 3x2 + 6x = x2 Combine like terms\n3x2 + 11 x + 5 = x2 Make equation equal zero\n− x2 − x2 Subtract x2 from both sides\n2 x2 + 11 x + 5 = 0 Factor\n(2 x + 1)( x + 5) = 0 Set each factor equal to zero\n2 x + 1 = 0 or x + 5 = 0 Solve each equation\n− 1 − 1 − 5 − 5 \n2 x = − 1 or x = − 5 \n2 2 \nx = − 1 \n2 \nor − 5 Check solutions, LCD can′t be zero\n− 1 \n2 \n+ 2 = 3\n2 \n− 5 + 2 = − 3 Neither make LCD zero, both are solutions\nx = − 1 \n2 \nor − 5 Our Solution\nThe LCD can be several factors in these problems. As the LCD ge ts more com-\nplex, it is important to remember the process we are using to s olve is still the\nsame. \nExample 368. \nx\nx + 2 \n+ 1 \nx + 1 = 5 \n( x + 1)( x + 2) Multiply terms by LCD, ( x + 1)( x + 2) \nx( x + 1)( x + 2) \nx + 2 + 1( x + 1)( x + 2) \nx + 1 = 5( x + 1)( x + 2) \n( x + 1)( x + 2) Reduce fractions\n275"
            }
          ],

        {
          "id": "SEC_49",
          "chapter_id": "CH_8",
          "number": "7.8",
          "title": "Application: Dimensional Analysis",
          "order": 48,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 279,
              "page_end": 279,
              "text": "7.8\nRational Expressions - Dimensional Analysis\nObjective: Use dimensional analysis to preform single unit , dual unit, \nsquare unit, and cubed unit conversions. \nOne application of rational expressions deals with convert ing units. When we con-\nvert units of measure we can do so by multiplying several frac tions together in a\nprocess known as dimensional analysis. The trick will be to d ecide what fractions\nto multiply. When multiplying, if we multiply by 1, the value of the expression\ndoes not change. One written as a fraction can look like many d iﬀerent things as\nlong as the numerator and denominator are identical in value . Notice the numer-\nator and denominator are not identical in appearance, but ra ther identical in\nvalue. Below are several fractions, each equal to one where n umerator and denom-\ninator are identical in value. \n1 \n1 \n= 4\n4\n= \n1 \n2 \n2 \n4\n= 100cm\n1 m = 1 lb\n16oz = 1 hr\n60 min = 60 min\n1 hr\nThe last few fractions that include units are called convers ion factors. We can\nmake a conversion factor out of any two measurements that rep resent the same\ndistance. For example, 1 mile = 5280 feet. We could then make a conversion\nfactor 1 mi\n5280ft because both values are the same, the fraction is still equal to one. \nSimilarly we could make a conversion factor 5280ft\n1 mi . The trick for conversions will\n279\nbe to use the correct fractions. \nThe idea behind dimensional analysis is we will multiply by a fraction in such a\nway that the units we don’t want will divide out of the problem . We found out\nwhen multiplying rational expressions that if a variable ap pears in the numerator\nand denominator we can divide it out of the expression. It is t he same with units. \nConsider the following conversion. \nExample 371. \n17.37 miles to feet Write 17.37 miles as a fraction, put it over1 ( 17.37mi\n1 \n)\nTo divide out the miles we need miles in the denominator\n( 17.37mi\n1 \n)( ?? ft\n?? mi\n)\nWe are converting to feet, so this will go in the numerator\n( 17.37mi\n1 \n)( 5280ft\n1 mi\n)\nFill in the relationship described above, 1 mile = 5280 feet\n( 17.37\n1 \n)( 5280ft\n1 \n)\nDivide out the miles and multiply across\n91 , 713.6ft Our Solution\nIn the previous example, we had to use the conversion factor 5280ft\n1 mi so the miles\nwould divide out. If we had used 1 mi\n5280ft we would not have been able to divide out\nthe miles. This is why when doing dimensional analysis it is v ery important to\nuse units in the set-up of the problem, so we know how to correc tly set up the\nconversion factor. \nExample 372. \nIf 1 pound = 16 ounces, how many pounds 435 ounces? \n( 435oz\n1 \n)\nWrite 435 asa fraction, put it over1 \n( 435oz\n1 \n)( ?? lbs\n?? oz\n)\nTo divide out oz, \nput it in the denominator and lbs in numerator( 435oz\n1 \n)( 1 lbs\n16oz\n)\nFill in the given relationship, 1 pound = 16 ounces\n280"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_49"
          ],

    {
      "id": "CH_9",
      "number": "8",
      "title": "Radicals",
      "order": 8,
      "sections": [
        {
          "id": "SEC_50",
          "chapter_id": "CH_9",
          "number": "8.1",
          "title": "Square Roots",
          "order": 49,
          "source_spans": [
            {
              "source_file_id": "SRC_1",
              "source_name": "Beginning_and_Intermediate_Algebra.pdf",
              "page_start": 288,
              "page_end": 288,
              "text": "8.1 \nRadicals - Square Roots\nObjective: Simplify expressions with square roots. \nSquare roots are the most common type of radical used. A squar e root “un-\nsquares” a number. For example, because 5 2 = 25 we say the square root of 25 is 5. \nThe square root of 25 is written as 25\n√\n. \nWorld View Note: The radical sign, when ﬁrst used was an R with a line\nthrough the tail, similar to our perscription symbol today. The R came from the\nlatin, “radix”, which can be translated as “source” or “foun dation”. It wasn’t until\nthe 1500s that our current symbol was ﬁrst used in Germany (bu t even then it\nwas just a check mark with no bar over the numbers! \nThe following example gives several square roots: \nExample 377. \n1 \n√\n= 1 121 \n√\n= 11 \n4\n√\n= 2 625\n√\n= 25\n9\n√\n= 3 − 81 √ = Undeﬁned\nThe ﬁnal example, − 81 √ is currently undeﬁned as negatives have no square root. \nThis is because if we square a positive or a negative, the answ er will be positive. \nThus we can only take square roots of positive numbers. In ano ther lesson we will\ndeﬁne a method we can use to work with and evaluate negative sq uare roots, but\nfor now we will simply say they are undeﬁned. \nNot all numbers have a nice even square root. For example, if w e found 8\n√\non\nour calculator, the answer would be 2.8284271247461900976 03377448419... and\neven this number is a rounded approximation of the square roo t. To be as accu-\nrate as possible, we will never use the calculator to ﬁnd deci mal approximations of\nsquare roots. Instead we will express roots in simplest radi cal form. We will do\nthis using a property known as the product rule of radicals\nProduct Rule of Square Roots: a · b\n√\n= a√ · b\n√\nWe can use the product rule to simplify an expression such as 3 6 · 5 \n√\nby spliting\nit into two roots, 36\n√\n· 5 \n√\n, and simplifying the ﬁrst root, 6 5 \n√\n. The trick in this\n288\nprocess is being able to translate a problem like 180\n√\ninto 36 · 5 \n√\n. There are sev-\neral ways this can be done. The most common and, with a bit of pr actice, the\nfastest method, is to ﬁnd perfect squares that divide evenly into the radicand, or\nnumber under the radical. This is shown in the next example. \nExample 378. \n75\n√\n75 is divisible by 25, a perfect square\n25 · 3\n√\nSplit into factors\n25\n√\n· 3\n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of 25\n5 3\n√\nOur Solution\nIf there is a coeﬃcient in front of the radical to begin with, t he problem merely\nbecomes a big multiplication problem. \nExample 379. \n5 63\n√\n63 is divisible by9, a perfect square\n5 9 · 7\n√\nSplit into factors\n5 9\n√\n· 7\n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of9\n5 · 3 7\n√\nMultiply coeﬃcients\n15 7\n√\nOur Solution\nAs we simplify radicals using this method it is important to b e sure our ﬁnal\nanswer can be simpliﬁed no more. \nExample 380. \n72\n√\n72 is divisible by9, a perfect square\n9 · 8\n√\nSplit into factors\n9\n√\n· 8\n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of9\n3 8\n√\nBut 8 is also divisible bya perfect square, 4\n3 4 · 2 \n√\nSplit into factors\n3 4\n√\n· 2 \n√\nProduct rule, take the square root of4\n3 · 2 2 \n√\nMultiply\n289"
            }
          ],
          "competency_ids": [
            "COMP_50"
          ],
          "lesson_goal": "Learn Square Roots well enough to explain the idea, solve a straightforward problem, and recognize when it should be used later in the course.",
          "learning_objectives": [],
